<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:introduction-main}
Let $M$ be a spherically complete model of $\mathrm{ACVF}$, and let $\mu$ be a
global $M$-invariant Keisler measure. The following conditions are
equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}
\item $\mu$ is stably
dominated over $M$;
\item $\mu$ is generically stable over $M$;
\item $\mu$ is orthogonal to the value group over $M$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{thm:introduction-correspondence}
Let $M\models\mathrm{ACVF}$ be spherically complete, suppose that $\Gamma(M)=(\mathbb R,+,\leq,0),$
and let $X$ be a projective variety defined over $M$. There is a canonical bijection between Radon probability measures on $X^{\mathrm{an}}$ and global
$M$-invariant generically stable Keisler measures concentrating on
$X$.
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{theo: intro radon and smooth in R}
Let $M$ be an o-minimal expansion of $(\mathbb R_\infty, +, \leq, 0)$. 
\begin{enumerate}
\item If $V$ is an $\mathbb R_{\infty}$-definable set contained in a closed box, and $\mu$ a countably additive Keisler measure on $V$ over $\mathbb R_{\infty}$ (in the sense of \cite[Definition $6.1$]{genmeasures}), then $\mu$ is smooth. In particular, the Keisler measure on the $\mathbb R_{\infty}$-definable subsets of a box $B$ induced by a Borel measure on $B$ is smooth.
\item Conversely, if $\mu$ is a smooth measure over $(\mathbb R_\infty)^k$ concentrating in a closed box $B$, there is a $\sigma$-additive $\mathbb R_{\infty}$-Borel measure on $B(\mathbb R_\infty)$ such that $\mu_0$ induces $\mu$ as a Keisler measure.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
\label{def:distal-type}
Let \(A\subseteq \mathbb M\) be small.
\begin{enumerate}
    \item Let \(p(x),q(y)\in S(A)\). We say that \(p\) and \(q\) are \emph{weakly orthogonal} if
    $ p(x)\cup q(y)$
    determines a complete type over \(A\).

    \item Let \(p(x)\) and \(q(y)\) be global \(M\)-invariant types. We say that \(p\) and \(q\) are \emph{orthogonal} if they are weakly orthogonal as global types.                  
                    
                                       
   \item If \(p\) is a global \(A\)-invariant type. We say that \(p\) is \emph{distal over \(A\)} if, for every tuple \(d\) and every Morley sequence
$I\models p^{\otimes \omega}\upharpoonright_{A d}$
the types $
p\upharpoonright_{A I}$ and $
\operatorname{tp}(d/A I)$
are weakly orthogonal.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
\label{def:stable-set-stably-embedded}
Let \(B\subseteq \mathbb M\) be definable.  
\begin{enumerate}
 \item A formula \(\phi(\bar x;\bar y)\) is \emph{stable}, if there are no sequences
    $(a_i,b_i)_{i\in \mathbb N}$
    with $a_i,b_i$ tuples in $\mathbb M$ such that
    $\mathbb M\models \phi(a_i;b_j)$ if and only if $i<j.$ The formula $\phi$ might have parameters.
  \item We say that \(B\) is \emph{stable in \(\mathbb M\)} if, for every formula \(\phi(x;y)\), there are no sequences
    $(a_i,b_i)_{i\in \mathbb N}$
    with \(a_i,b_i\in B\) such that
    $\mathbb M\models \phi(a_i;b_j)$ if and only if $i<j.$ 
    \item We say that \(B\) is \emph{stably embedded in \(\mathbb M\)} if for every formula \(\phi(x_1,\dots,x_n;y)\), there is a formula \(\psi(x_1,\dots,x_n;z)\), depending only on \(\phi\), such that for every \(b\in \mathbb M^y\), there is \(d\in B^z\) satisfying
    $ \phi(B^n,b)=\psi(B^n,d).$
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- fact-1: body_md -->
\cite[Proposition 1,5]{OnPe}\label{OnPe}
    $\theta(x)$ is stable as a partial type if and only if the definable set $\theta(\mathbb M)$ is stable and stably embedded.
<!-- end fact-1 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
\cite[Definition~3.1]{HHM}.\label{def: stable part of the structure} Let $A=\mathrm{acl}^{eq}(A)$ be a small set of imaginary parameters. We denote by $\mathrm{St}_{A}$  the multi-sorted structure $(\mathrm{D}_{i}, \mathrm{R}_{j})_{i \in \mathrm{I}, j \in \mathrm{J}}$ whose sorts $\mathrm{D}_{i}$ are the $A$-definable, stable, stably embedded subsets of $\mathbb M$. For each finite set of sorts $\mathrm{D}_i$, all the $A$-definable relations on their finite product are included as $\emptyset$-definable relations $\mathrm{R}_{j}$.
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- fact-2: body_md -->
\label{fact: stablepartproperties} The structure $\mathrm{St}_{A}$ is stable, stably embedded over $A$ and eliminates imaginaries.
<!-- end fact-2 -->
<!-- notation-1: body_md -->
\label{not:notationstabletrace}
For every parameter set $B$, we put $\mathrm{St}_A(B)
:=
\mathrm{St}_A \cap\mathrm{dcl}^{eq}(A B).$
<!-- end notation-1 -->
<!-- definition-4: body_md -->
Let \(A\subseteq \mathbb M\) be a small set of parameters. A \emph{Keisler measure over \(A\)} in the variable \(x\) is a finitely additive probability measure on $\mathbb{B}(A)$: the Boolean algebra of \(\mathcal L_x(A)\)-definable subsets of \(\mathbb M^x\). A \emph{global Keisler measure} is a Keisler measure over \(\mathbb M\).
<!-- end definition-4 -->
<!-- definition-5: body_md -->
\label{def:smooth}
Let \(M\models T\), and let \(\mu\) be a Keisler measure over \(M\).
\begin{enumerate}
    \item We say that \(\mu\) is \emph{smooth} if it has a unique extension to a Keisler measure over every model \(N\supseteq M\).
    \item A global Keisler measure \(\mu\) is said to be \emph{smooth over \(M\)} if \(\mu\upharpoonright_{\mathcal L_x(M)}\) is smooth.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end definition-5 -->
<!-- definition-6: body_md -->
\label{def:invariant-definable-fs}
Let \(A\subseteq \mathrm{B}\subseteq \mathbb M\) be small sets of parameters. Let \(\mu\) be a Keisler measure over $\mathrm{B}$.
\begin{enumerate}
    \item We say that \(\mu\) is \emph{\(A\)-invariant} if, for every formula \(\phi(x,y)\) and all tuples \(b,b'\in \mathrm{B}^y\), whenever
    $b\equiv_{A} b',$
    we have
    $\mu(\phi(x,b))=\mu(\phi(x,b')).$

    \item We say that \(\mu\) is \emph{finitely satisfiable in \(A\)} if, for every formula \(\phi(x,y)\) and every \(b\in \mathrm{B}^y\), whenever
    $ \mu(\phi(x,b))>0,$
    there is some \(a\in A^x\) such that
    $ \mathbb M\models \phi(a,b).$

    \item We say that $\mu$ is \emph{nonforking over $A$} if for any formula \(\phi(x,y)\) and every \(b\in \mathrm{B}^y\) if $\phi(x,b)$ forks over $A$ then $\mu(\phi(x,b))=0$.

    \item We say that \(\mu\) is \emph{definable over \(A\)} if \(\mu\) is \(A\)-invariant and for every formula \(\phi(x,y)\) and every \(r\in [0,1]\), the set $\{q\in S_y(A) \ | \  \mu(\phi(x,b))<r \text{ for any $b\in \mathrm{B}$ with $b\models q$}\}$ is open in \(S_y(A)\).

\item We say that \(\mu\) is \emph{Borel definable over \(A\)} if \(\mu\) is \(A\)-invariant and for every formula \(\phi(x,y)\) and every \(r\in [0,1]\), the set
\[\{q\in S_y(A) \ | \  \mu(\phi(x,b))<r \text{ for any $b\in \mathrm{B}$ with $b\models q$}\}, \ \text{is Borel in \(S_y(A)\)}.\]

\item We write $\mu\upharpoonright_{A}$ to denote the restriction of $\mu$ to $\mathbb{B}(A)$: the boolean algebra of $A$-definable subsets. We say that $\mu \upharpoonright_{A}$ is \emph{stationary} if it has a unique non-forking global extension.  
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end definition-6 -->
<!-- fact-3: body_md -->
\label{fact:support-keisler-measure}
Let $A$ be a small set of parameters. Let $\mu$ be a Keisler measure over
$A$. Define
\[
\operatorname{Supp}(\mu)
:=
\left\{
p\in S_x(A) \ | \ 
\mu(\theta(x))>0
\text{ for every }\theta(x)\in p
\right\}.\]
Then $\operatorname{Supp}(\mu)$ is a closed, and hence compact,
subset of $S_x(A)$. Moreover, for every formula $\theta(x)\in\mathcal L_x(A)$,
$\mu(\theta(x))>0$ if and only if $[\theta(x)]\cap\operatorname{Supp}(\mu)\neq\varnothing.$
<!-- end fact-3 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
This is \cite[Section~7.1, p.~99]{simon}, we include details for sake of completeness. A type $p\in S_x(A)$ does not belong to
$\operatorname{Supp}(\mu)$ if and only if there is a formula
$\theta(x)\in p$ such that
$\mu(\theta(x))=0.$
Consequently,
$S_x(A)\setminus\operatorname{Supp}(\mu)=\bigcup_{\mu(\theta)=0}[\theta],$
where the union ranges over all $\mathcal L_x(A)$-formulas of
$\mu$-measure zero. Each set $[\theta]$ is clopen in the Stone
topology, so the complement of $\operatorname{Supp}(\mu)$ is open.
Thus $\operatorname{Supp}(\mu)$ is closed.  Since $S_x(A)$ is compact,
the support is compact.\\
For the second part of the statement, if $p\in[\theta]\cap\operatorname{Supp}(\mu),$
then $\theta\in p$, and the definition of the support gives
$\mu(\theta)>0.$ Conversely, suppose that
$\mu(\theta)>0$
and, toward a contradiction, that
$[\theta]\cap\operatorname{Supp}(\mu)=\varnothing.$
For every $p\in[\theta]$, choose a formula $\psi_p(x)\in p$ such
that
$\mu(\psi_p(x))=0.$
The clopen sets $[\psi_p]$, as $p$ ranges over $[\theta]$, form an
open cover of the compact set $[\theta]$. Hence there are
$p_1,\ldots,p_n\in[\theta]$ such that
$
[\theta]
\subseteq
\bigcup_{i=1}^n[\psi_{p_i}].
$
Equivalently,
$
\models
\forall x\,
\left(
\theta(x)\rightarrow
\bigvee_{i=1}^n\psi_{p_i}(x)
\right).
$
By monotonicity and finite subadditivity,
$
\mu(\theta)
\leq
\sum_{i=1}^n\mu(\psi_{p_i})
=
0,
$
contradicting $\mu(\theta)>0$. Therefore
$
[\theta]\cap\operatorname{Supp}(\mu)\neq\varnothing.
$
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{thm:genstable}
Let \(A\subseteq \mathbb M\) be small, and let \(\mu\) be a global \(A\)-invariant Keisler measure. Then the following are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \(\mu\) is definable over \(A\) and finitely satisfiable in some small model containing \(A\);
    \item \(\mu^\omega_{(x_0,x_1,\dots)}\upharpoonright_A\) is totally indiscernible;
                                       
    \item for every global \(A\)-invariant Keisler measure \(\lambda_y\), one has
    $\mu_x\otimes \lambda_y=\lambda_y\otimes \mu_x;$
    \item \(\mu\) commutes with itself, namely
    $\mu_x\otimes \mu_y=\mu_y\otimes \mu_x;$
    \item for some small model \(M_0\supseteq A\), for every \(X\subseteq S_x(A)\) Borel  set  over $A $, and every formula \(\phi(x)\) over \(\mathbb M\), if
    $ \mu(X\cap \phi(x))>0,$
    then there is \(a\in M_0^x\) such that \(a\in X\) and
    $\mathbb M\models \phi(a).$
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- definition-7: body_md -->
\label{def:generically-stable-global-measure}
Let \(A\subseteq \mathbb M\) be small set of parameters. Let \(\mu\) be a global \(A\)-invariant Keisler measure. We say that \(\mu\) is \emph{generically stable over \(A\)} if it satisfies one, equivalently all, of the conditions of Theorem~\ref{thm:genstable}.
<!-- end definition-7 -->
<!-- fact-4: body_md -->
\label{fact:global-invariant-extension}
Assume that $T$ is $\mathrm{NIP}$. Every Keisler measure over a model $M$ admits
a global $M$-invariant extension.
<!-- end fact-4 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
A Keisler measure over $M$ does not fork over $M$. Hence the result is
\cite[Lemma~5.2]{genmeasures}.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- fact-5: body_md -->
\label{fact:smoothmeasurezero} Let $\lambda$ be a smooth Keisler measure over $M$.
And let $p$ be a non-realized type over $M$. Then $\widetilde{\lambda}(p)=0$, where $\widetilde{\lambda}$ is the Borel measure on space of types over $M$ induced by $\lambda$.
<!-- end fact-5 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
By \cite[Lemma $4.1$]{SimonGenericallyStable} the localization at $\{p\}$, namely the Dirac measure
$\delta_p$, is smooth over $M$.
If $p$ is not realized in $M$, it has distinct
realizations $a,a'\in\mathbb U$.
The Dirac measures $\delta_a$ and $\delta_{a'}$ give distinct
global extensions of $\delta_p$, but then $\delta_{p}$ is not smooth.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- notation-2: body_md -->
For a fixed tuple of variables $x$ and a parameter set $A\subseteq\mathbb M$, we will use $\mathbb B_x(A)$ to denote the Boolean algebra of $\mathcal L_x(A)$-formulas modulo
logical equivalence in $\mathbb M$. We identify a formula with its
equivalence class in this Boolean algebra. When the tuple of variables
is clear, we simply write $\mathbb B(A)$. 


For any Boolean sub-algebra $\mathbb F\subseteq\mathbb B_x(\mathbb M)$  and
any global Keisler measure $\mu$ we write $\mu\upharpoonright_{\mathbb F}$
for the restriction of $\mu$ to $\mathbb F$. For any small model $M$ of $\mathbb M$ we will write $\mu\upharpoonright_{M}$ to indicate the restriction of $\mu$ to $\mathcal{L}_{x}(M)$.
<!-- end notation-2 -->
<!-- definition-8: body_md -->
\label{def:F-approximation}
Let $\mu$ be a global Keisler measure and let
$\mathbb F\subseteq\mathbb B_x(\mathbb M)$ be a Boolean subalgebra.

\begin{enumerate}
\item Let $\phi(x,b)$ be an $\mathcal L_x(\mathbb M)$-formula. We say that
$\phi(x,b)$  is
\emph{$\mathbb F$-approximated with respect to $\mu$} if, for every
$\varepsilon>0$, there are formulas
$\theta_-(x),\theta_+(x)\in\mathbb F$
such that
$\mathbb M\models
\forall x\,
\bigl(
\theta_-(x)\rightarrow
\phi(x,b)\rightarrow
\theta_+(x)
\bigr)$
and
$|\mu(\theta_+(x))-\mu(\theta_-(x))|<\varepsilon.$

\item We say that $\mu$ is \emph{$\mathbb F$-approximated} if every
$\mathcal L_x(\mathbb M)$-formula is $\mathbb F$-approximated with respect
to $\mu$.

\item For an $\mathcal L_x(\mathbb M)$-formula $\phi(x,b)$, we define its
\emph{inner $\mathbb F$-measure} and
\emph{outer $\mathbb F$-measure} by
\begin{align*}
\mu_{\mathrm{in}}^{\mathbb F}(\phi(x,b))
&:=
\sup
\left\{
\mu(\theta(x)):
\begin{array}{l}
\theta(x)\in\mathbb F,\\
\mathbb M\models
\forall x\,
\bigl(
\theta(x)\rightarrow\phi(x,b)
\bigr)
\end{array}
\right\},\\
\mu_{\mathrm{out}}^{\mathbb F}(\phi(x,b))
&:=
\inf
\left\{
\mu(\theta(x)):
\begin{array}{l}
\theta(x)\in\mathbb F,\\
\mathbb M\models
\forall x\,
\bigl(
\phi(x,b)\rightarrow\theta(x)
\bigr)
\end{array}
\right\}.
\end{align*}
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end definition-8 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{lem:unique-extension-criterion}
Let $\mu$ be a global Keisler measure, let
$\mathbb F\subseteq\mathbb B_x(\mathbb M)$ be a Boolean subalgebra, and let
$\phi(x,b)$ be an $\mathcal L_x(\mathbb M)$-formula. The following are
equivalent.

\begin{enumerate}
\item The formula $\phi(x,b)$ is $\mathbb F$-approximated with
respect to $\mu$.

\item One has
$\mu_{\mathrm{in}}^{\mathbb F}(\phi(x,b))=\mu_{\mathrm{out}}^{\mathbb F}(\phi(x,b)).$

\item The value of $\phi(x,b)$ is uniquely determined by
$\mu\upharpoonright_{\mathbb F}$: whenever $\nu_{1}$ and $\nu_{2}$ are  global Keisler measures satisfying $\nu_{1}\upharpoonright_{\mathbb F}=\mu\upharpoonright_{\mathbb F}=\nu_{2} \upharpoonright_{\mathbb{F}}$ one has $\nu(\phi(x,b))=\mu(\phi(x,b)).$
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
Let $\nu$ be any
Keisler measure defined on a Boolean algebra containing both
$\mathbb F$ and $\phi(x,b)$, and suppose that
$\nu\upharpoonright_{\mathbb F}=\mu\upharpoonright_{\mathbb F}.$
If $\theta(x)\in\mathbb F$ and
$\mathbb M\models
\forall x\,
\bigl(
\theta(x)\rightarrow\phi(x,b)
\bigr),$
then monotonicity of $\nu$ gives
$\mu(\theta(x))=\nu(\theta(x))\leq\nu(\phi(x,b)).$
Taking the supremum over all such $\theta$ yields
$\mu_{\mathrm{in}}^{\mathbb F}(\phi(x,b))\leq\nu(\phi(x,b)).$
Similarly, if $\theta(x)\in\mathbb F$ and
$\mathbb M\models
\forall x\,
\bigl(
\phi(x,b)\rightarrow\theta(x)
\bigr),$
then
$\nu(\phi(x,b))\leq\nu(\theta(x))=\mu(\theta(x)).$
Taking the infimum gives
$\nu(\phi(x,b))\leq \mu_{\mathrm{out}}^{\mathbb F}(\phi(x,b)).$
Thus every extension satisfies
\[\mu_{\mathrm{in}}^{\mathbb F}(\phi(x,b))
\leq
\nu(\phi(x,b))
\leq
\mu_{\mathrm{out}}^{\mathbb F}(\phi(x,b)).
\tag{$\star$}\]
We now prove the equivalences.\\
\noindent
\emph{$(1)\rightarrow(2)$.}
Assume that $\phi(x,b)$ is $\mathbb F$-approximated with respect to $\mu$. Let
$\varepsilon>0$. Choose
$
\theta_-(x),\theta_+(x)\in\mathbb F
$
such that
$\theta_-(x)\rightarrow\phi(x,b)\rightarrow\theta_+(x)$
and $\mu(\theta_+(x))-\mu(\theta_-(x))<\varepsilon.$
By the definitions of inner and outer measure,
$\mu_{\mathrm{in}}^{\mathbb F}(\phi(x,b))\geq\mu(\theta_-(x))$ and $\mu_{\mathrm{out}}^{\mathbb F}(\phi(x,b))\leq\mu(\theta_+(x)).$
Consequently,
$0
\leq
\mu_{\mathrm{out}}^{\mathbb F}(\phi(x,b))-\mu_{\mathrm{in}}^{\mathbb F}(\phi(x,b)) \leq
\mu(\theta_+(x))-\mu(\theta_-(x))<
\varepsilon.$
Since $\varepsilon>0$ was arbitrary, we obtain
$\mu_{\mathrm{in}}^{\mathbb F}(\phi(x,b))=\mu_{\mathrm{out}}^{\mathbb F}(\phi(x,b)).$

\smallskip

\noindent
\emph{$(2)\rightarrow(1)$.}
Assume that $(2)$ holds and let $r=\mu_{\mathrm{in}}^{\mathbb F}(\phi(x,b))=\mu_{\mathrm{out}}^{\mathbb F}(\phi(x,b))$ Applying
\textup{$\star$} to the original measure $\mu$ gives
$\mu_{\mathrm{in}}^{\mathbb F}(\phi(x,b))\leq\mu(\phi(x,b))\leq \mu_{\mathrm{in}}^{\mathbb F}(\phi(x,b)).$
Hence $\mu(\phi(x,b))=r.$ Let $\varepsilon>0$. By the definition of the supremum, there is
$\theta_-(x)\in\mathbb F$ such that
$\theta_-(x)\rightarrow\phi(x,b)$ and
$\mu(\theta_-(x))>r-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.$
Similarly, by the definition of the infimum, there is
$\theta_+(x)\in\mathbb F$ such that
$\phi(x,b)\rightarrow\theta_+(x)$
and
$\mu(\theta_+(x))<r+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.$\\
Therefore,
$\mu(\theta_+(x))-\mu(\theta_-(x))<\left(r+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right)-\left(r-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right)=\varepsilon.$
Thus $\phi(x,b)$ is $\mathbb F$-approximated with respect to $\mu$.

\smallskip

\noindent
\emph{$(2)\rightarrow(3)$.}
Let  $r=\mu_{\mathrm{in}}^{\mathbb F}(\phi(x,b))=\mu_{\mathrm{out}}^{\mathbb F}(\phi(x,b))$. Let $\nu_1$ and $\nu_2$ be global
extensions of $\mu\upharpoonright_{\mathbb F}$. By \textup{($\star$)}, for
$i=1,2$,
$
r=\mu_{\mathrm{in}}^{\mathbb F}(\phi(x,b))
\leq
\nu_i(\phi(x,b))
\leq
\mu_{\mathrm{out}}^{\mathbb F}(\phi(x,b))=r.
$
Since $\mu_{\mathrm{in}}^{\mathbb F}(\phi(x,b))=\mu_{\mathrm{out}}^{\mathbb F}(\phi(x,b))$, both values must be equal to $r$. Hence
$\nu_1(\phi(x,b))=\nu_2(\phi(x,b)).$

\smallskip

\noindent
\emph{$(3)\rightarrow(2)$.}
Suppose, toward a contradiction, that
$
\mu_{\mathrm{in}}^{\mathbb F}(\phi(x,b))< \mu_{\mathrm{out}}^{\mathbb F}(\phi(x,b)).
$
Choose two distinct real numbers
$
r_1,r_2\in[\mu_{\mathrm{in}}^{\mathbb F}(\phi(x,b)),\mu_{\mathrm{out}}^{\mathbb F}(\phi(x,b))].
$
By \cite[Lemma~1.3.(iv)]{Keisler}, for each $i=1,2$ there is an extension
$\nu_i$ of $\mu\upharpoonright_{\mathbb F}$ to a global Keisler measure such
that
$
\nu_i(\phi(x,b))=r_i.
$
Thus
$
\nu_1(\phi(x,b))
\neq
\nu_2(\phi(x,b)),
$
contradicting $(3)$. Therefore $\mu_{\mathrm{in}}^{\mathbb F}(\phi(x,b))=\mu_{\mathrm{out}}^{\mathbb F}(\phi(x,b))$.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{prop: inner and outer measures}
Let $\mu$ be a global measure, let $A\subset \mathbb M$ be a small set, let
$\mathbb F\subseteq\mathbb B_x(\mathbb M)$ be a Boolean subalgebra containing $\mathbb B_x(A)$, and let $\mu^{\mathbb F}$ be the restriction of $\mu$ to $\mathbb F$. 

Then the following hold.

\begin{enumerate}
\item If $\mu^{\mathbb F}$ is $A$-definable, then $\mu_{\mathrm{out}}^{\mathbb F}$ is $A$-definable.

\item If $\mu^{\mathbb F}$ is finitely satisfiable over $A$, then $\mu_{\mathrm{inn}}^{\mathbb F}$ is finitely satisfiable over $A$.

\item If $\mu$ is $\mathbb F$-approximated, then $\mu$ is $A$-definable/finitely satisfiable over $A$ if and only if $\mu^{\mathbb F}$ is $A$-definable/finitely satisfiable over $A$.\end{enumerate}
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
The third item follows from the first two since \cref{lem:unique-extension-criterion} implies that if $\mu$ is $\mathbb F$-approximated then $\mu$ is equal to both $\mu_{\mathrm{inn}}^{\mathbb F}$ and $\mu_{\mathrm{out}}^{\mathbb F}$ on any definable set.


We now prove $(1)$. Let $\phi(x,w)$ be a $\mathcal{L}$-formula, $r \in [0,1]$ and let 
\[O=\{ q \in S_{y}(A) \ | \ \mu_{\mathrm{out}}^{\mathbb F}(\phi(x,b'))< r  \ \text{for all $b' \models q$}\}.\] We want to show that $O$ is an open set. Fix an element $b \in \mathbb{M}$ and let $q_{0}=\operatorname{tp}(b/A) \in O$. By definition of the outer measure, there is a formula $\theta_{+}(x,d) \in \mathbb F$ such that $\phi(x,b) \rightarrow \theta_{+}(x,d)$ and  $\mu^\mathbb F(\theta_{+}(x,d)) < r$. By hypothesis there is a formula $\eta(z) \in tp(d/A)$ such that for any $\mathbb{M} \models \eta(d')$ we have that $\mu^\mathbb F\big( \theta_+(x,d')\big) < r$.\\ 

Let $\phi(w):=\exists z \big( \eta(z) \wedge \forall x\big( \phi(x,w)\rightarrow \theta_+(x,z)\big).$
Note that $\phi(w) \in \operatorname{tp}(b/A)$ and for any $b'$ such that $\mathbb{M} \models \phi(b')$ then there is $\mathbb{M}\models \eta(d')$ such that $\phi(x,b')\rightarrow \theta_+(x, d')$ and $\mu^\mathbb F(\theta_+(x,d'))<r$. By definition,  $\mu_{\mathrm{out}}^{\mathbb F}\big(\phi(x,b')\big)<r$. Hence $q_{0} \in [\phi(w)] \subseteq O$, thus $O$ is open. 


For $(2)$, we want to show that if $\mu^{\mathbb{F}}$ is  is finitely satisfiable over $M$, so it is $\mu^\mathbb F_{\mathrm{inn}}$. Assume that $\mu_{\mathrm{inn}}^{\mathbb F}(\phi(x,b))>0$ for a fixed parameter $b \in \mathbb{M}$. By definition of the inner measure there is a formula $\theta_{-}(x,d) \in \mathbb F$ such that $\mathbb{M} \models \forall x \big( \theta_{-}(x,d) \rightarrow \phi(x,b))$ and $\mu^\mathbb F(\theta_{-}(x,d))>0$. Since $\mu^\mathbb F(\theta_{-}(x,d))$ was finitely satisfiable there is some $m \in M$ such that $\mathbb{\mathbb M} \models \theta_{-}(m,d)$. Hence $\mathbb{\mathbb M} \models \phi(m,b)$, as required.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- fact-6: body_md -->
\label{fact:uniquenessmeasures} Let $(X, \mathcal{F})$ be a measurable space and $\mathcal{P} \subseteq \mathcal{F}$. And assume that:
\begin{enumerate}
\item the set $X \in \mathcal{P}$,
\item $\mathcal{P}$ is closed under finite intersections,
\item the $\sigma$-algebra generated by $\mathcal{P}$ is $\mathcal{F}$.
\end{enumerate}
If $\mu$ and $\nu$ are finitely countably additive measures on $\mathcal{F}$ such that $\mu(A)=\nu(A)$ for all $A \in \mathcal{P}$ then $\mu(B)=\nu(B)$ for all $B \in \mathcal{F}$.
<!-- end fact-6 -->
<!-- fact-7: body_md -->
\label{fact:acvf-stably-embedded-sorts}
In \(\mathrm{ACVF}\), the residue field \(\mathbf k\) is a stably embedded pure algebraically closed field and eliminates imaginaries. The value group \(\Gamma\) is a stably embedded pure divisible ordered abelian group and eliminates imaginaries. In particular, \(\Gamma\) is distal.
<!-- end fact-7 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
The stable embeddedness and induced structures on \(\mathbf k\) and \(\Gamma\) are standard consequences of quantifier elimination for \(\mathrm{ACVF}\); see \cite[Proposition $7.1$ and $7.2$]{HHM}. The residue field is a model of \(\mathrm{ACF}\), and the value group is a model of the theory of divisible ordered abelian groups. The value group is distal because divisible ordered abelian groups are o-minimal; see \cite[Corollary $2.30$]{distal}.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- notation-3: body_md -->
\label{not:residue-value-prodefinable-maps}
Let \(M\models \mathrm{ACVF}\) be a spherically complete model. For a tuple \(a\) from the valued-field sort, we write
$\mathbf f(a)$
for a pro-definable enumeration of the residue-field part
$\mathbf k(Ma)\colon=\mathbf k(\mathbb{M}) \cap \mathrm{dcl}^{eq}(Ma)$
and
$\mathbf g(a)$
for a pro-definable enumeration of the value-group part
$\Gamma(Ma)\colon=\Gamma(\mathbb{M}) \cap \mathrm{dcl}^{eq}(Ma).$
Equivalently, \(\mathbf f\) and \(\mathbf g\) are \(M\)-pro-definable maps which record, respectively, the residue-field and value-group data generated by \(a\) over \(M\). If \(p\) is a global \(M\)-invariant type, we write
$p^{st}:=\mathbf f_*p$
for its residue-field projection, and
$p^\Gamma:=\mathbf g_*p$
for its value-group projection. Note that both $p^{st}$ and $p^{\Gamma}$ are still $M$-invariant.
<!-- end notation-3 -->
<!-- fact-8: body_md -->
\label{fact:implication-from-residue-value-restrictions} Let $M \models \mathrm{ACVF}$ be a spherically complete model. 
Let \(p\) be a global \(M\)-invariant type in the valued-field sort, and let \(b\in K(\mathbb M)\). Let \(\mathbf f\) and \(\mathbf g\) denote the residue-field and value-group projection maps from \cref{not:residue-value-prodefinable-maps}. If \(a\models p\upharpoonright_M\), then
$a\models p\upharpoonright_{Mb}$
if and only if
$\mathbf f(a)\models p^{st}\upharpoonright_{Mb}$ and $
\mathbf g(a)\models p^\Gamma\upharpoonright_{Mb}.$
Equivalently, $p\upharpoonright_{M} \cup p^{st} \upharpoonright_{Mb} \cup p^{\Gamma}\upharpoonright_{Mb} \vdash p \upharpoonright_{Mb}$.
<!-- end fact-8 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
The direction $a \models p \upharpoonright_{M b}$ implies that $\mathbf{f}(a) \models p^{st}\upharpoonright_{Mb}$ and $\mathbf{g}(a) \models p^{\Gamma} \upharpoonright_{Mb}$ is immediate. For the converse, assume $\mathbf{f}(a)=\mathbf{k}(Ma) \models p^{st} \upharpoonright_{Mb}$. In particular, $\mathbf{k}(Ma)\downfree_{M} Mb$ hence $\mathbf{k}(Ma) \downfree_{\mathbf{k}(M)} \mathbf{k}(Mb)$ (\emph{c.f.} \cite[Lemma $2.6$]{orthogonal}). Hence, $\mathbf{k}(Ma)$ and $ \mathbf{k}(Mb)$ are algebraically independent over $\mathbf{k}(M)$.  Since $\mathbf{k}(M)$ is algebraically closed, the residue field $\mathbf{k}(Ma)$ is linearly disjoint from $\mathbf{k}(Mb)$ over $\mathbf{k}(M)$. Similarly,  $\mathbf{g}(a) \models p^{\Gamma} \upharpoonright_{Mb}$ implies $\Gamma(Ma) \downfree_{\Gamma(M)} \Gamma(Mb)$. In particular, $\Gamma(Ma) \cap \Gamma(Mb)=\Gamma(M)$. (\emph{e.g.} by \cite[Remark $3.1$ and Fact $2.5$]{Vic-ResDom}). By \cite[Corollary 12.11]{HHM}.(i) (or \cite[Proposition 3.3]{Vic-ResDom}), 
\[\operatorname{tp}(a/M) \cup \operatorname{tp}(\mathbf{k}(Ma)/ \mathbf{k}(M b)) \cup \operatorname{tp}(\Gamma(Ma)/ \Gamma(Mb)) \vdash \operatorname{tp}(a/Mb)\]
Hence $p\upharpoonright_{M} \cup p^{st} \upharpoonright_{Mb} \cup p^{\Gamma}\upharpoonright_{Mb} \vdash p \upharpoonright_{Mb}$. \\
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{lem:finite-gamma-normal-form} Let $T$ be $\mathrm{NIP}$ and $M \models T$. Let $\Gamma$ be a $\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}$-structure. Assume that $\Gamma$ is a $\emptyset$-definable set in $M$ that is stably embedded, eliminates imaginaries and its induced structure is the $\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}$-structure. Let $X$ be an $M$-definable set, and let
$\varphi(x;y)$ be an $\mathcal L(M)$-formula, where $x$ ranges over $X$ and $y$ ranges over $\Gamma^m$. Then there exist an $M$-definable set $S\subseteq\Gamma^r,$ an $M$-definable map $g:X\mapsto S,$
and a $\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}$-formula
$\theta(u;y)$, with
parameters from $\Gamma(M)$, such that
$T\models
\forall x\in X\,\forall y\in\Gamma^m\,
\bigl(
\varphi(x;y)
\leftrightarrow
\theta(g(x);y)
\bigr).$
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
Consider the $M$-definable equivalence relation on $X$ given by
$a\sim a'$ if and only if $\forall y\in\Gamma^m\,
\bigl(
\varphi(a;y)\leftrightarrow\varphi(a';y)
\bigr).$ 
Thus two elements of $X$ are equivalent precisely when they define
the same $\phi$-subset of $\Gamma^m$.
Since $\Gamma$ is stably embedded  and it eliminates imaginaries, there is a $\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}$-formula $\theta(y;u)$, depending only on $\varphi$, such that
each set $\varphi(a;\Gamma^m)$ is of the form $\theta(\Gamma^m;c_{a})$ for some tuple $c_{a}$ from $\Gamma \cap \mathrm{dcl}^{eq}(Ma)$. Indeed, elimination of imaginaries allows one to take the
canonical parameter of the set $\Theta(\Gamma^m;c)$  in a $M$-definable subset $S$ of a finite Cartesian power of $\Gamma$. By compactness, we can make such coice of canonical parameters uniform, and we obtain a map $g\colon X \to S$ where each element $a$ is sent to the canonical parameter $c_{a}$, such function clearly factors through the quotient $X/\sim$. By construction, $g(a)$ is the canonical parameter of the subset $\Theta(g(a);\Gamma^{m})$, which coincides with 
$\varphi(a;\Gamma^m)$. Therefore
$\varphi(a;y)\leftrightarrow\theta(g(a);y)$
for every $a\in X$ and $y\in\Gamma^m$, which proves the main statement.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- definition-9: body_md -->
\label{def:stablebooleanalgebra} Let $B \supseteq A=\mathrm{acl}^{eq}(A)$ be a small set of parameters.
\begin{enumerate}
\item A \emph{special pushforward stable formula} is a formula of the form: $\psi(x,a) \wedge \alpha(f(x),d)$, where $\psi(x,a)$ is a $\mathcal{L}(A)$-formula, $f: \psi(\mathbb M,a)\to D$ is an $A$-definable map, $D$ is a finite product of sorts in $\mathrm{St}_{A}$ and $\alpha(u,d)$ is a formula in the stably embedded structure $\mathrm{St}_{A}$ where $d \in \mathrm{St}_{A}(B)$.
\item The \emph{stable Boolean algebra over $A$ with parameters from $B$,} denoted $\mathrm{DEF\text{-}ST}_A(B),$ is the Boolean sub-algebra of $\mathbb B_x(B)$ generated by special pushforward stable formulas. 
\item If $\mu$ is a global Keisler measure, we write
$\mu_{\mathrm{st},A}
:=
\mu\upharpoonright_{\mathrm{DEF\text{-}ST}_A(\mathbb M)}.$ When the base is fixed, we simply write $\mu_{\mathrm{st}}$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end definition-9 -->
<!-- definition-10: body_md -->
\label{def:stable-approximation-and-domination}
Let $\mu$ be a measure on $\mathbb B_x(B)$ for $B\supseteq A$.

\begin{enumerate}
\item We say that $\mu$ is \emph{stably approximated over $A$} if
$\mu$ is $\mathrm{DEF\text{-}ST}_A(B)$-approximated in the sense of
Definition~\ref{def:F-approximation}.

\item If $\mu$ is a global Keisler measure, we say that $\mu$ is \emph{stably dominated over $A$} if
$\mu_{\mathrm{st},A}$ has a unique extension to the full Boolean
algebra $\mathbb B_x(\mathbb M)$, and this unique extension is $\mu$.
\end{enumerate}
Note that the notions in $(1)$ and $(2)$ are equivalent by \cref{lem:unique-extension-criterion}.
<!-- end definition-10 -->
<!-- definition-11: body_md -->
\label{def:thestablepushforward} Let $\mu$ be a global Keisler measure and $A=\mathrm{acl}^{eq}(A)$ be a small algebraically closed base of imaginary parameters. Let $\psi(x,a)$ be a $\mathcal{L}(A)$-formula and let $f$ be an $A$-definable map  $f: \psi(x,a) \rightarrow D$, where $D$ is a finite product of sorts in $\mathrm{St}_{A}$. Let $\nu$ be the localized measure of $\mu$ on the definable set $\psi(x,a)$. We define the \emph{stable push-forward of $\mu$ on $\psi(x,a)$ along $f$} to be the pushforward measure of $\nu$ along $f$, which we denote by $\mu_{f}$. More explicitly, for $\alpha(u,d)$ a formula within the stable structure $\mathrm{St}_{M}(\mathbb{M})$, where $d \in \mathrm{St}_{M}(\mathbb{M})$ and $u$ is a variable varying in $f(\psi(x,a)) \subseteq \mathrm{St}_{M}(\mathbb{M})$, we define:
\[ \mu_{f} \big( \alpha(u,d)\big)= \nu\big(\alpha(f(x),d)\big)=\mu \big( \psi(x,a) \wedge \alpha(f(x),d)\big).\]
<!-- end definition-11 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
\label{rmk:thestablepushforward} The stable pushforward of $\mu$ on $\psi(x,a)$ along $f$ defined in \cref{def:thestablepushforward} is a Keisler measure on a stable and stably embedded $A$-definable set $D$, centered on the definable subsets of $f(\psi(x,a)) \subseteq D$. Since $\mu$ is $A$-invariant, $\psi(x,a)$ is a $\mathcal{L}(A)$-formula and $f$ is $A$-definable then the pushforward of $\mu$ on $\psi(x,a)$ along $f$ is also $A$-invariant. In particular it is generically stable over $A$ so it is definable and finitely satisfiable over $A$ (\emph{c.f.} \cref{thm:genstable}).
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{lem: babycontinuity} Let $A=\mathrm{acl}^{eq}(A)$ and  $\mu$ be a global $A$-invariant Keisler measure. Let $\psi(x,a)$ be a $\mathcal{L}(A)$-formula, $f: \psi(x,a) \to D$ where $D$ is a finite product of sorts in $\mathrm{St}_{A}$. Let $\alpha(u,z)$ be a $\mathcal{L}(A)$-formula in the stable structure $\mathrm{St}_{A}(\mathbb{M})$. 
Then:
\begin{enumerate}
\item for every $r \in [0,1]$, then set \[O:=\{ q \in S_{yz}(A) \ | \ \mu(\psi(x,a) \wedge \alpha(f(x),d')) < r \ \text{for any $(a,d')\in \mathbb{M}$ with $(a,d') \models q$} \}\]
is open. 
\item Furthermore, if the base $A$ is a model $M$, then given $d \in \mathrm{St}_{M}(\mathbb{M})$ a fixed parameter, if $\mu(\psi(x,a) \wedge \alpha(f(x),d))>0$ then there is $m \in M$ such that $\mathbb{M} \models \psi(m,a) \wedge \alpha(f(m),d)$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
Let $q_{0}=\operatorname{tp}(a,d/M)$, where $d \in \mathrm{St}_{A}(\mathbb{M})$. Let $\mu_{f}$ be the pushfoward measure of $\mu$ on $\psi(x,a)$ along $f$, as in \cref{def:thestablepushforward}. By \cref{rmk:thestablepushforward} such pushforward is $A$-invariant and generically stable over $A$, so it is $A$-definable. In particular, for a fixed $r \in [0,1]$ the set $\{ p \in S_{z}(A) \ |\ \mu_{f}(\alpha(u,d'))<r \ \text{for any $d' \models p$} \ \}$ is an open set, so there is a formula $\delta(z) \in \operatorname{tp}(d/A)$ such that for every $d' \in \mathrm{St}_{M}(\mathbb{M})$ we have  $\mu_{f}(\alpha(u,d'))<r$ whenever $\mathbb{M}\models \delta(d')$. Let $\eta(y,z)\colon = \big(y=a \wedge \delta(z)\big)$.  Then $\eta(y,z) \in q_{0}$ and by the definition of the stable pushforward $\mu\big( \psi(x,a) \wedge \alpha(f(x),d')\big)=\mu_{f}\big(\alpha(u,d')\big)< r$ for any $\mathbb{M} \models \eta(a',d')$. 
Thus $q_{0} \in [\eta(y,z)]$ and $O$ is an open set. \\

For the second part of the statement, note that by definition of the stable pushforward of $\mu$ on $\psi(x,a)$ along $f$, we have that $\mu_{f}(\alpha(u,d))=\mu(\psi(x,a) \wedge \alpha(f(x),d))>0$. By \cref{rmk:thestablepushforward} the stable pushforward of $\mu$ on $\psi(x,a)$ along $f$ is finitely satisfiable over $\mathrm{St}_{M}(M)\preceq \mathrm{St}_{M}(\mathbb{M})$). Hence, there is some $n_{0} \in \mathrm{St}_{M}(M)$ such that $\mathbb{M}\models \alpha(n_{0},d)$.  Since $f: \psi(x,a) \rightarrow f(\psi(x,a)) \subseteq D \subseteq \mathrm{St}_{A}(\mathbb{M})$, and $\alpha(u,d)$ is a definable subset of $f(\psi(x,a))$, we have that $f$ is surjective. Take $m_{0} \in M$ be such that $f(m_{0})=n_{0}$, hence $\mathbb{M} \models \psi(m_{0},a) \wedge \alpha(f(m_{0}),d)$, as required.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{prop:stableapproxthengenstable} Let $T$ be a $\mathrm{NIP}$-theory. Let $M$ be a small model and $\mu$ a global $M$-invariant Keisler measure. If $\mu$ is stably dominated over $M$ then $\mu$ is generically stable.
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
By \cref{prop: inner and outer measures}, the result follows from \cref{lem: babycontinuity}.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- definition-12: body_md -->
\label{def:measure-orthogonal-gamma}
Let $\mu$ be a global $A$-invariant Keisler measure, and let $\Gamma$ be a stably embedded set. We say that
$\mu$ is \emph{orthogonal to $\Gamma$} if, for every finite
tuple
$
b\in\Gamma(\mathbb M)^n,
$
the measure $\mu\upharpoonright_A$ has a unique extension to $Ab$.
<!-- end definition-12 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
\label{thm:main-generically-stable-measures-acvf}
Let $M$ be a spherically complete model of $\mathrm{ACVF}$, and let $\mu$ be a global $M$-invariant
Keisler measure. The following are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}
\item $\mu$ is stably approximated over $M$/ $\mu$ is stably dominated over $M$,
\item $\mu$ is generically stable over $M$;
\item $\mu$ is orthogonal to the value group over $M$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
The implication $(1) \rightarrow (2)$ holds in any theory by \cref{prop:stableapproxthengenstable}. 

$(2) \rightarrow (3)$ will  hold for an arbitrary model of $\mathrm{ACVF}$. Indeed, its proof requires that the theory is $\mathrm{NIP}$ and that $\Gamma$ is a stably embedded set whose induced theory is distal. Our proof of the implication $(3)\rightarrow (1)$ only holds in $\mathrm{ACVF}$, and it does use spherically complete hypothesis on the base $M$ since it uses \cref{fact:implication-from-residue-value-restrictions}.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{fact:strong-definitions-gamma} Let $T$ be $\mathrm{NIP}$ and $M \models T$. Let $\Gamma$ be an $\emptyset$-definable set equipped with $\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}$-structure, and assume that $\Gamma$ is stably embedded, eliminates imaginaries and its induced structure is the $\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}$-structure which is distal.
Let $\phi(x;y)$ be an
$\mathcal L(M)$-formula in which $y$ ranges over $\Gamma^n$, and let $b\in\Gamma(\mathbb M)^n$. Then there are an elementary extension of pairs $(\mathbb M,M)\preceq(\mathbb M',M'),$ $\mathcal L(M)$-formulas $\psi_0(x;z)$ and $\psi_1(x;z)$, and a tuple
$d\in M'$ such that
$\mathbb M'\models
\forall x\,
\bigl(
\psi_0(x;d)\rightarrow
\phi(x;b)\rightarrow
\psi_1(x;d)
\bigr)$ and $\psi_0(M;d)
=\phi(M;b)=\psi_1(M;d).$
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
By \cref{lem:finite-gamma-normal-form} there are an
$M$-definable set
$S\subseteq\Gamma^m,$
an $M$-definable surjective map
$\rho:X \rightarrow S,$
and an $\mathcal L_\Gamma(\Gamma(M))$-formula $\chi(y;u)$, with
$u$ ranging over $S$, such that
$\mathbb M\models
\forall x\,\forall y\,
\bigl(
\phi(x;y)\leftrightarrow
\chi(y;\rho(x))
\bigr).$  For $a\in X(\mathbb M)$, the element $\rho(a)$ is the canonical
parameter of the subset
$\{c\in\Gamma(\mathbb M)^n | \ \mathbb M\models\phi(a;c)\}.$
Let
$A_\rho:=\rho(X(M))\subseteq S(\Gamma(\mathbb M)).$
Thus $A_\rho$ is the set of canonical parameters of the members of
the family defined by taking instances of $\phi$, whose parameters range on $M$.
Consider the structure
$\bigl(\Gamma(\mathbb M),A_\rho\bigr),$
namely the distal structure induced on $\Gamma(\mathbb M)$  expanded by a
predicate for the subset $A_\rho$.  The set $A_\rho$ is definable in the pair
$(\mathbb M,M)$, since
$
u\in A_\rho \leftrightarrow
\exists x\,
\bigl(
P(x)\wedge\rho(x)=u
\bigr),
$
where $P$ is the predicate naming the distinguished sub-model $M$ of $\mathbb{M}$. Consequently, the structure $(\Gamma(\mathbb M),A_{\rho})$ is interpretable in $(\mathbb M, M)$. \\
Choose a sufficiently saturated elementary extension of pairs $(\mathbb M,M)\preceq(\mathbb M',M').$ Let $A_\rho':=\rho(X(M')).$
By interpretability,
$\bigl(\Gamma(\mathbb M),A_\rho\bigr)
\preceq
\bigl(\Gamma(\mathbb M'),A_\rho'\bigr)$
is an elementary extension. We choose $(\mathbb M',M')$ sufficiently
saturated so $\bigl(\Gamma(\mathbb M'),A_\rho'\bigr)$ is also sufficiently saturated.  In particular, we can apply the argument in the proof \cite[Theorem~9.21]{simon} to the formula
$
\widetilde\phi(u;y)
:=
\chi(y;u),$
the subset $A_\rho\subseteq S(\Gamma(\mathbb M))$, and the tuple
$b\in\Gamma(\mathbb M)^n$, and we obtain an $\mathcal L_\Gamma(\Gamma(M))$-formula
$\Theta(y;z)$ and a tuple
$d\in A_\rho'\subseteq M'$
such that $\Gamma(\mathbb M')\models\Theta(b;d)$
and $\Theta(y;d)$ determines the
$\widetilde\phi^{\mathrm{opp}}$-type of $b$ over $A_\rho$. The last assertion states that for every $u\in A_\rho$ one has
$\Theta(y;d)\vdash\chi(y;u),$ if $\Gamma(\mathbb M)\models\chi(b;u),$ and 
$\Theta(y;d)\vdash\neg\chi(y;u),$ if $\Gamma(\mathbb M)\models\neg\chi(b;u).$ Given $a\in X(M)$ then $\rho(a)\in A_\rho$. In particular, for every $a\in X(M)$,
\begin{align}\label{eq:check}
\Theta(y;d)&\vdash\phi(a;y), \ \text{if} \ \mathbb M\models\phi(a;b), \ \text{and}\\
\Theta(y;d)&\vdash\neg\phi(a;y), \ \text{if} \ \mathbb M\models\neg\phi(a;b).
\end{align}
We now construct the required lower and upper approximations.\\
Let
$\psi_0(x;z)
:=\forall y\,
\bigl(
\Theta(y;z)\rightarrow\phi(x;y)
\bigr),$ and $
\psi_1(x;z)
:=
\exists y\,
\bigl(
\Theta(y;z)\wedge\phi(x;y)
\bigr).$
Since $\Theta$ is an $\mathcal L_\Gamma(\Gamma(M))$-formula and
$\phi$ is a $\mathcal{L}(M)$-formula, both $\psi_0$ and $\psi_1$ are
$\mathcal L(M)$-formulas. \\
Since the tuple $b$ satisfies $\Theta(y;d)$. Then, for every $a\in X(\mathbb M')$, if $\mathbb M' \models \psi_0(a;d) $ then $\mathbb M' \models \phi(a;b),$
because the universal implication defining $\psi_0(a;d)$ may be
evaluated at $y=b$. Similarly, if 
$\mathbb M' \models \phi(a;b)$ then $\mathbb M' \models \psi_1(a;d),$
because $y=b$ witnesses the existential quantifier defining
$\psi_1(a;d)$. Thus
$
\mathbb M'\models
\forall x\,
\bigl(
\psi_0(x;d)\rightarrow
\phi(x;b)\rightarrow
\psi_1(x;d)
\bigr),
$ which proves the first part of the statement.\\
It is only left to compare the traces on $M$. Let $a\in X(M)$. Suppose first that
$\mathbb M\models\phi(a;b).$
By  \textup{(1)}, $\Theta(y;d)\vdash\phi(a;y).$
Therefore
$\mathbb M'\models\psi_0(a;d).$
Together with the fact that 
$\mathbb{M} \models \forall x \big( \psi_0(x;d)\rightarrow\phi(x;b)\big)$, this shows that
$\psi_0(M;d)=\phi(M;b).$
Now suppose that $\mathbb M\models\neg\phi(a;b).$
By \textup{(2)}, $\Theta(y;d)\vdash\neg\phi(a;y).$
Hence there is no $y$ satisfying both $\Theta(y;d)$ and
$\phi(a;y)$, so $\mathbb M'\models\neg\psi_1(a;d).$
Since $\mathbb{M} \models \forall x \big( \phi(x;b)\rightarrow\psi_1(x;d)\big)$, we can conclude that
$\phi(M;b)=\psi_1(M;d).$ We conclude that
$\psi_0(M;d)=\phi(M;b)=\psi_1(M;d),$
which is the second part of the statement.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{prop:gs-implies-gamma-orthogonal} Let $T$, $M$ and $\Gamma$ be as in \cref{fact:strong-definitions-gamma}. Let $\mu$ be a global $M$-invariant Keisler measure. If $\mu$ is
generically stable over $M$, then $\mu$ is orthogonal to $\Gamma$.
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
Let $b\in\Gamma(\mathbb M)^n.$ We prove that $\mu\upharpoonright_M$ has a unique extension to $Mb$.
Fix an $\mathcal L(M)$-formula $\phi(x;y)$, where $y$ ranges over
$\Gamma^n$, and let $\varepsilon>0$. By
Fact~\ref{fact:strong-definitions-gamma}, there are an elementary
extension of pairs
$
(\mathbb M,M)\preceq(\mathbb M',M'),
$
formulas $\psi_0(x;z)$ and $\psi_1(x;z)$ over $M$, and a tuple
$d\in M'$ such that
\[\mathbb M'\models
\forall x,
\bigl(
\psi_0(x;d)\rightarrow
\phi(x;b)\rightarrow
\psi_1(x;d)
\bigr),  \ \text{and}
\tag{*}\]

\[\psi_0(M;d)
= \phi(M;b)
=\psi_1(M;d).
\tag{**}\]

Since $\mu$ is generically stable over $M$, it is definable over $M$
and finitely satisfiable in $M$ (\emph{c.f} \cref{thm:genstable}).  We write $\mu$ to also indicate the canonical extension (i.e. the definable extension which is unique by \cite[Theorem~A.24]{CoGaHa}) of $\mu$ to $\mathbb{M}'$, which is still generically stable over $M$. 
We first show that the two strong definitions in \textup{(*)} have
the same $\mu$-measure as $\phi(x;b)$.  Put $r:=\mu(\phi(x;b)).$  \\

\begin{claim} \emph{$\mu(\psi_{0}(x,d))=\mu(\psi_{1}(x,d))=\mu(\phi(x,b))=r$}.\end{claim}
\begin{claimproof}
Consider $D_0(x):=\phi(x;b)\wedge\neg\psi_0(x;d).$
By \textup{(**)}, the formula $D_0(x)$ has no realization in $M$.
If
$\mu(D_0(x))>0,$
finite satisfiability of $\mu$ in $M$ would give some $a\in M^x$
satisfying $D_0(a)$, a contradiction. Hence $\mu\bigl(
\phi(x;b)\setminus\psi_0(x;d)
\bigr)
=0.$ Similarly, the formula
$D_1(x):=
\psi_1(x;d)\wedge\neg\phi(x;b)$
has no realization in $M$, again by \textup{(**)}. Therefore
$\mu\bigl(
\psi_1(x;d)\setminus\phi(x;b)
\bigr)=0.$
Combining this with \textup{(*)} we conclude that $
\mu(\psi_0(x;d))
= \mu(\phi(x;b))
= \mu(\psi_1(x;d))=r.$ \end{claimproof}

It remains to replace the parameter $d\in M'$ by parameters from
$M$. For $i=0,1$, consider the function
$F_i:S_z(M)\mapsto [0,1],$ defined as $
F_i(\operatorname{tp}(c/M)):=\mu(\psi_i(x;c)).$
Since $\mu$ is definable over $M$, the function $F_i$ is continuous.
By Claim~1,
$F_i(\operatorname{tp}(d/M))=r.$
Therefore the inverse image under $F_i$ of the interval
$(r-\varepsilon/2,r+\varepsilon/2)$
is an open neighborhood of $\operatorname{tp}(d/M)$ in $S_z(M)$. Since the Stone
topology has a basis of clopen sets, there is an
$\mathcal L(M)$-formula $\theta_i(z)\in\operatorname{tp}(d/M)$ such that
\[
\left|
\mu(\psi_i(x;c))-r
\right|
<
\frac{\varepsilon}{2} \ \text{whenever}\ \mathbb M'\models\theta_i(c).\]

In $(\mathbb M',M')$, the tuple $d\in M'$ satisfies
$\theta_0(d)\wedge\forall x\bigl(\psi_0(x;d)\rightarrow\phi(x;b)\bigr).$
Writing $P$ for the predicate naming the distinguished submodel, this
says that
$(\mathbb M',M')\models\exists z\big(P(z)\wedge\theta_0(z)\wedge\forall x\bigl(\psi_0(x;z)\rightarrow\phi(x;b)\big).$
The parameters occurring in this formula belong to the smaller pair
$(\mathbb M,M)$. Since
$
(\mathbb M,M)\preceq(\mathbb M',M'),
$
there is $d_0\in M$ such that
$\mathbb M\models
\forall x
\bigl(
\psi_0(x;d_0)\rightarrow\phi(x;b)
\bigr)$
and, $\left|
\mu(\psi_0(x;d_0))-r
\right|
<
\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.$
Applying the same argument to
$
\theta_1(z)
\wedge
\forall x
\bigl(
\phi(x;b)\rightarrow\psi_1(x;z)
\bigr),
$
we obtain $d_1\in M$ such that
$\mathbb M\models
\forall x
\bigl(
\phi(x;b)\rightarrow\psi_1(x;d_1)
\bigr)$
and
$\left|
\mu(\psi_1(x;d_1))-r
\right|
<
\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.$

Consequently, 
$\mathbb M \models \forall x \big(\psi_0(x;d_0)
\rightarrow
\phi(x;b)
\rightarrow
\psi_1(x;d_1)\big).$
In particular,
$\mu(\psi_0(x;d_0))
\leq r
\leq
\mu(\psi_1(x;d_1)).$
Hence
\[
\mu(\psi_1(x;d_1))
-\mu(\psi_0(x;d_0))
=
\bigl(
\mu(\psi_1(x;d_1))-r
\bigr)
+
\bigl(
r-\mu(\psi_0(x;d_0))
\bigr)\
<
\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
+
\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\
=
\varepsilon.
\]

Thus, for the arbitrary formula $\phi(x;b)$ and every
$\varepsilon>0$, we have found $M$-definable lower and upper
approximations whose measures differ by less than $\varepsilon$. By
Lemma~\ref{lem:unique-extension-criterion}, the measure
$\mu\upharpoonright_M$ has a unique extension to $Mb$. Since the tuple $b\in\Gamma(\mathbb M)^n$ was arbitrary, $\mu$ is orthogonal
to $\Gamma$.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- claim-1: body_md -->
\emph{$\mu(\psi_{0}(x,d))=\mu(\psi_{1}(x,d))=\mu(\phi(x,b))=r$}.
<!-- end claim-1 -->
<!-- claimproof-1: body_md -->
Consider $D_0(x):=\phi(x;b)\wedge\neg\psi_0(x;d).$
By \textup{(**)}, the formula $D_0(x)$ has no realization in $M$.
If
$\mu(D_0(x))>0,$
finite satisfiability of $\mu$ in $M$ would give some $a\in M^x$
satisfying $D_0(a)$, a contradiction. Hence $\mu\bigl(
\phi(x;b)\setminus\psi_0(x;d)
\bigr)
=0.$ Similarly, the formula
$D_1(x):=
\psi_1(x;d)\wedge\neg\phi(x;b)$
has no realization in $M$, again by \textup{(**)}. Therefore
$\mu\bigl(
\psi_1(x;d)\setminus\phi(x;b)
\bigr)=0.$
Combining this with \textup{(*)} we conclude that $
\mu(\psi_0(x;d))
= \mu(\phi(x;b))
= \mu(\psi_1(x;d))=r.$
<!-- end claimproof-1 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{lem:weakly-random-invariant-extension} Let $M \models \mathrm{ACVF}$ and $\mu$ be a global Kiesler $M$-invariant measure.
Let $b$ be a finite tuple from $\mathbb M$, and let
$p\in S_x(Mb)$ assume that
$\mu(\theta(x))>0$
for every $\mathcal{L}(Mb)$-formula $\theta(x)\in p$. Then $p$ does not fork over $M$.
Consequently, $p$ admits a global $M$-invariant extension.
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
Since $\mathrm{ACVF}$ is dependent and $\mu$ is $M$-invariant, the measure $\mu$
does not fork over $M$ (\emph{c.f.} \cite[Proposition 4.3]{nipmeasures}). In particular, every formula of positive
$\mu$-measure does not fork over $M$. Let $\theta(x)\in p$. By assumption,
$\mu(\theta(x))>0.$
Therefore $\theta(x)$ does not fork over $M$. As this holds for every
formula in $p$, the type $p$ does not fork over $M$. Let $q$ be a global non-forking extension of $p$. In a dependent theory, a
global type which does not fork over a model is invariant over that
model (\emph{c.f.}\cite[Proposition $2.1(ii)$]{genmeasures}). Hence $q$ is a global $M$-invariant extension of $p$.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- definition-13: body_md -->
\label{def:stable-value-neighborhood}  Let $M$ be a spherically complete model of $\mathrm{ACVF}$. 
Let $q$ be a global $M$-invariant type in the valued-field variables
$x$, and let $b$ be a finite tuple from the valued-field sort.

A formula $\vartheta(x)\in q\upharpoonright_{Mb}$ is called a
\emph{stable--value group neighborhood of $q$ over $Mb$} if it can be
written as
$\vartheta(x)
=
\psi(x)
\wedge
\alpha\bigl(f(x);d\bigr)
\wedge
\beta\bigl(g(x);e\bigr),$
where:

\begin{enumerate}
\item $\psi(x)$ is an $\mathcal L(M)$-formula belonging to
$q\upharpoonright_M$;
\item $D$ is a finite product of sorts of $\mathrm{St}_M$, and
$f:\psi(\mathbb M)\mapsto D$
is an $M$-definable map;
\item $d\in\mathrm{St}_M(Mb)$
and $\alpha(u;d)$ is a formula in the multi-sorted stable structure
$\mathrm{St}_M$;
\item for some $r<\omega$,
$g:\psi(\mathbb M)\mapsto\Gamma^r$
is an $M$-definable map;
\item $e\in\Gamma(Mb)$
and $\beta(v;e)$ is a formula in the pure language of ordered
divisible abelian groups.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end definition-13 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{lem:existence-stable-value-neighborhood} Let $M$ be a spherically complete model of $\mathrm{ACVF}$. 
Let $q$ be a global $M$-invariant type in the valued-field variables,
let $b$ be a finite tuple from the valued-field sort, and let
$\phi(x;b)\in q.$
Then there is a stable--value group neighborhood $\vartheta_q(x)$ of $q$
over $Mb$ such that $\vartheta_q(x)\rightarrow\phi(x;b).$
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
By \cref{fact:implication-from-residue-value-restrictions}, $q\upharpoonright_{M} \cup q^{st} \upharpoonright_{Mb} \cup q^{\Gamma}\upharpoonright_{Mb} \vdash q \upharpoonright_{Mb}$. 
Since $\phi(x;b)\in q\upharpoonright_{Mb}$, by compactness there are  finitely many
formulas from these three partial types already imply
$\phi(x;b)$. Let $\psi(x)$ be the conjunction of the finitely many formulas chosen
from $q\upharpoonright_M$. Only finitely many coordinates of the stable and
value-group pro-definable maps occur in the remaining formulas (\emph{c.f.} \cref{not:residue-value-prodefinable-maps}).
After including their domains in $\psi(x)$, these coordinates give
$M$-definable maps $f:\psi(\mathbb M)\mapsto D$ and $g:\psi(\mathbb M)\mapsto\Gamma^r,$ where $D$ is a finite product of sorts of $\mathrm{St}_M$.
Since $\mathrm{St}_{M}$ is stably embedded and eliminates imaginaries (\cref{fact: stablepartproperties}), the finite conjunction of
the chosen stable formulas in $q^{st}\upharpoonright_{Mb}$ may be written as
$\alpha(f(x);d)$ for some $d\in\mathrm{St}_M(Mb).$
Similarly, stable embeddedness and elimination of imaginaries in the value group (\emph{c.f.} \cref{fact:acvf-stably-embedded-sorts}), allow the
finite conjunction of the chosen value-group formulas to be written
as
$\beta(g(x);e)$ for some $e\in\Gamma(Mb),$
where $\beta$ is a formula in the pure ordered-group language.

Put
$\vartheta_q(x)
:=
\psi(x)
\wedge
\alpha(f(x);d)
\wedge
\beta(g(x);e).$
Each conjunct belongs to $q$, so
$\vartheta_q(x)\in q.$
By the choice of the finite subsets, one also has
$\vartheta_q(x)\rightarrow\phi(x;b).$
Thus $\vartheta_q$ is the required stable--value group neighborhood.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{lem:stable-value-neighborhood-approximated}
Assume that $\mu$ is orthogonal to $\Gamma$. Following the notation introduced in \cref{def:stable-value-neighborhood}. Let
$\vartheta_{q}(x)
=
\psi_{q}(x)
\wedge
\alpha_{q}(f(x);d)
\wedge
\beta_{q}(g(x);e)$
be a stable--value group neighborhood of a global $M$-invariant type $q$. Then
$\vartheta_{q}(x)$ is $\mathrm{DEF\text{-}ST}_{M}(\mathbb{M})$-approximated with respect to $\mu$. More precisely, for every $\varepsilon>0$, there are
$\vartheta^{q}_-(x),\vartheta^{q}_+(x)\in \mathrm{DEF\text{-}ST}_{M}(\mathbb{M})$ 
such that
$\vartheta^{q}_-(x)
\rightarrow
\vartheta_{q}(x)
\rightarrow
\vartheta^{q}_+(x)$
and
$\mu(\vartheta^{q}_+(x))-\mu(\vartheta^{q}_-(x))<\varepsilon.$
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
Let
$\rho_{q}(x;e):=\psi_{q}(x)\wedge\beta_{q}(g(x);e).$
The only parameter of $\rho(x;e)$ outside $M$ is the finite tuple $e\in\Gamma(Mb).$
Since $\mu$ is orthogonal to $\Gamma$, the measure
$\mu\upharpoonright_M$ has a unique extension to $Me$. By
Lemma~\ref{lem:unique-extension-criterion} (applied to $\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{B}_{x}(M)$), for every
$\varepsilon>0$ there are $\mathcal L(M)$-formulas
$\rho^{q}_-(x)$ and $\rho^{q}_+(x)$ such that
$\rho^{q}_-(x)
\rightarrow
\rho_{q}(x;e)
\rightarrow
\rho^{q}_+(x)$ and
$\mu(\rho^{q}_+(x))-\mu(\rho^{q}_-(x))<\varepsilon.$
Define
$\vartheta^{q}_-(x)
:=
\rho^{q}_-(x)\wedge\alpha_{q}(f(x);d)$
and
$\vartheta^{q}_+(x)
:=
\rho^{q}_+(x)\wedge\alpha_{q}(f(x);d).$
The formula $\alpha_{q}(f(x);d)$ is stable and $d \in \mathrm{St}_{M}(\mathbb{M})$,
and $\rho^{q}_-$ and $\rho^{q}_+$ are formulas over $M$. Hence
$\vartheta^{q}_-(x),\vartheta^{q}_+(x)\in \mathrm{DEF\text{-}ST}_{M}(\mathbb{M}).$
The implication $\vartheta^{q}_-(x) \rightarrow \vartheta_{q}(x)\rightarrow \vartheta^{q}_+(x)$ is immediate.  Moreover,
$\vartheta^{q}_+(x)\setminus\vartheta^{q}_-(x)
\subseteq
\rho^{q}_+(x)\setminus\rho^{q}_-(x).$
Therefore:
\[
\mu(\vartheta^{q}_+(x))-\mu(\vartheta^{q}_-(x))=
\mu\big(
\vartheta^{q}_+(x)\setminus\vartheta^{q}_-(x)
\big)\leq
\mu\big(
\rho^{q}_+(x)\setminus\rho^{q}_-(x)
\big)=
\mu(\rho^{q}_+(x))-\mu(\rho^{q}_-(x))<
\varepsilon.
\]
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{prop:stable-inner-approximation} Let $M$ be a spherically complete model of $\mathrm{ACVF}$ and let  $\mu$ be a global $M$-invariant measure that is orthogonal to $\Gamma$. Let $\phi(x;b)$ be an
arbitrary formula and let $\varepsilon>0$. Then there is a formula
$\sigma(x)\in\mathbb \mathrm{DEF\text{-}ST}_{M}(\mathbb M)$
such that
$\sigma(x)\rightarrow\phi(x;b)$
and $\mu(\phi(x;b))-\mu(\sigma(x))<\varepsilon.$
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
Let
$\mu_b:=\mu\upharpoonright_{Mb}$
and let
$
K_{\phi,b}
:=
\left\{
p\in\operatorname{Supp}(\mu_b):
\phi(x;b)\in p
\right\}.$
The support $\operatorname{Supp}(\mu_b)$ is closed in
$S_x(Mb)$, and hence $K_{\phi,b}$ is compact (\emph{c.f.} \cref{fact:support-keisler-measure}). Fix $p\in K_{\phi,b}.$
By \cref{lem:weakly-random-invariant-extension}, choose a global
$M$-invariant extension $q_p$ of $p$. Since
$\phi(x;b)\in q_p,$ by
\cref{lem:existence-stable-value-neighborhood} we obtain a stable-value neighborhood
$\vartheta_p(x)\in p$
such that
$\vartheta_p(x)\rightarrow\phi(x;b).$ The clopen sets
$\{[\vartheta_p] \ | \ p\in K_{\phi,b}\}$
cover $K_{\phi,b}$. By compactness, choose
$p_1,\ldots,p_n\in K_{\phi,b}$ such that
$K_{\phi,b}\subseteq \bigcup_{i=1}^n[\vartheta_{p_i}].$
Let $\eta(x):=\bigvee_{i=1}^n\vartheta_{p_i}(x).$
Then $\eta(x)\rightarrow\phi(x;b).$\\

\begin{claim}
\emph{$\mu(\phi(x;b)\wedge\neg\eta(x))=0.$}    
\end{claim} 
\begin{claimproof}
Suppose otherwise. Since
$\phi(x;b)\wedge\neg\eta(x)$ is a formula over $Mb$, we have
$\mu_b\bigl(\phi(x;b)\wedge\neg\eta(x)\bigr)>0.$
By Fact~\ref{fact:support-keisler-measure}, there is a type
$p\in\operatorname{Supp}(\mu_b)$
such that $\phi(x;b)\wedge\neg\eta(x)\in p.$
Since $\phi(x;b)\in p$, we have $p\in K_{\phi,b}.$
The finite cover constructed above therefore implies
$\eta(x)\in p$. On the other hand,
$\neg\eta(x)\in p$, a contradiction. This concludes the proof of the Claim.\end{claimproof}

For each $i\leq n$, apply
Lemma~\ref{lem:stable-value-neighborhood-approximated} to
$\vartheta_{p_i}(x)$ and  $\frac{\varepsilon}{n}$, to obtain
$\vartheta_i^-(x),\vartheta_i^+(x) \in\mathrm{DEF\text{-}ST}_{M}(\mathbb M)$
such that
$\vartheta_i^-(x)\rightarrow\vartheta_{p_i}(x)\rightarrow\vartheta_i^+(x)$
and
$\mu(\vartheta_i^+(x))-\mu(\vartheta_i^-(x))<\frac{\varepsilon}{n}.$
Put $\sigma(x):=\bigvee_{i=1}^n\vartheta_i^-(x).$
Then $\sigma\in \mathrm{DEF\text{-}ST}_{M}(\mathbb M)$ and, $\mathbb M \models \forall x \big(\sigma(x)\rightarrow \eta(x) \rightarrow \phi(x;b)\big).$ Moreover, $\eta(\mathbb M)\setminus\sigma(\mathbb M)
\subseteq
\bigcup_{i=1}^n
\big(
\vartheta_{p_i}(\mathbb M)\setminus\vartheta_i^-
(\mathbb M)\big).$ For each $i \leq n$, we have
$
\vartheta_{p_i}(\mathbb M)\setminus\vartheta_i^-
(\mathbb M)\subseteq
\vartheta_i^+(\mathbb M)\setminus\vartheta_i^-(\mathbb M),
$
and hence
$
\mu\big(
\vartheta_{p_i}(x)\setminus\vartheta_i^-(x)
\big)
<
\frac{\varepsilon}{n}.
$
By the claim and finite additivity, we conclude that
\[
\mu(\phi(x;b))-\mu(\sigma(x))
=
\mu(\eta(x))-\mu(\sigma(x))=
\mu(\eta(x)\wedge \neg \sigma(x))\leq
\sum_{i=1}^n
\mu\big(
\vartheta_{p_i}(x)\setminus\vartheta_i^-(x)
\big)<
\varepsilon.\]
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- claim-2: body_md -->
\emph{$\mu(\phi(x;b)\wedge\neg\eta(x))=0.$}
<!-- end claim-2 -->
<!-- claimproof-2: body_md -->
Suppose otherwise. Since
$\phi(x;b)\wedge\neg\eta(x)$ is a formula over $Mb$, we have
$\mu_b\bigl(\phi(x;b)\wedge\neg\eta(x)\bigr)>0.$
By Fact~\ref{fact:support-keisler-measure}, there is a type
$p\in\operatorname{Supp}(\mu_b)$
such that $\phi(x;b)\wedge\neg\eta(x)\in p.$
Since $\phi(x;b)\in p$, we have $p\in K_{\phi,b}.$
The finite cover constructed above therefore implies
$\eta(x)\in p$. On the other hand,
$\neg\eta(x)\in p$, a contradiction. This concludes the proof of the Claim.
<!-- end claimproof-2 -->
<!-- proposition-5: body_md -->
\label{prop:orthogonal-then-stably-dominated} Assume that $M$ is a spherically complete model of $\mathrm{ACVF}$. Let $\mu$ be a global $M$-invariant Keisler measure. If $\mu$ is
orthogonal to $\Gamma$, then $\mu$ is stably approximated over $M$.
Consequently, $\mu$ is stably dominated over $M$.
<!-- end proposition-5 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
Let $\phi(x;b)$ be a formula and let $\varepsilon>0$. By \cref{prop:stable-inner-approximation} applied to $\phi(x;b)$ and $\varepsilon/2$, we obtain
$\sigma_-(x)\in \mathrm{DEF\text{-}ST}_{M}(\mathbb M)$
such that
$\sigma_-(x)\rightarrow\phi(x;b)$
and $\mu(\phi(x;b))-\mu(\sigma_-(x))<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.$ Applying the same proposition to $\neg\phi(x;b)$ and $\varepsilon/2$, we obtain
$\tau(x)\in \mathrm{DEF\text{-}ST}_{M}(\mathbb M)$ such that
$\tau(x)\rightarrow\neg\phi(x;b)$
and
$\mu(\neg\phi(x;b))-\mu(\tau(x))<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.$
Set $\sigma_+(x):=\neg\tau(x).$
Since $\mathrm{DEF\text{-}ST}_{M}(\mathbb M)$ is a Boolean algebra,
$\sigma_+(x)\in \mathrm{DEF\text{-}ST}_{M}(\mathbb M)$. 
Moreover,
$\phi(x;b)\rightarrow\sigma_+(x),$
then  $\mu(\sigma_+(x))-\mu(\phi(x;b))<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.$
Combining the previous results, we get
$\sigma_-(x)\rightarrow\phi(x;b)\rightarrow\sigma_+(x)$
and
\[
\mu(\sigma_+(x))-\mu(\sigma_-(x))
=
\bigl(
\mu(\sigma_+(x))-\mu(\phi(x;b))
\bigr) +
\bigl(
\mu(\phi(x;b))-\mu(\sigma_-(x))
\bigr)
<\varepsilon.\]
Thus $\mu$ is $\mathrm{DEF\text{-}ST}_{M}(\mathbb M)$-approximated, and hence is stably
approximated over $M$.

The equivalence between stable approximation and stable domination from \cref{lem:unique-extension-criterion}, now shows
that $\mu$ is stably dominated over $M$.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
Any stably dominated measure is generically stable by \cref{prop:stableapproxthengenstable}, this is the implication $(1) \rightarrow (2)$. The implication $(2)\rightarrow (3)$, i.e from generic stability to orthogonality to the value
group is Proposition~\ref{prop:gs-implies-gamma-orthogonal}. Finally, the implication $(3)\rightarrow (1)$, this is from orthogonality to the value group to stable approximation is
\cref{prop:orthogonal-then-stably-dominated}. Lastly, stable
approximation is equivalent to stable domination by
\cref{lem:unique-extension-criterion}.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- fact-9: body_md -->
\label{borelandcountablyadditivethensmooth}\cite[Theorem 6.3]{genmeasures}
Let $M$ be an o-minimal expansion of the real field. Let $V$ be a definable set in $M$, and $\lambda$ be a countably additive Keisler measure on $V$ over $\mathbb R$. Then $\lambda$ is smooth.
<!-- end fact-9 -->
<!-- fact-10: body_md -->
\label{smooththenborel}\cite[Proposition 5.5]{SimonGenericallyStable}
Let $M$ be an o-minimal expansion of the real field. Let $\mu$ be a smooth measure over $M^k$ concentrating in $[0,1]^k$. Then there is $\mu_{0}$ a $\sigma$-additive $\mathbb R$-Borel measure on $[0,1]^k$ such that $\mu_0$ induces $\mu$ as a Keisler measure.
<!-- end fact-10 -->
<!-- theorem-6: body_md -->
\label{theo: radon and smooth in R}
Let $M$ be an o-minimal expansion of $(\mathbb R_\infty, +, \leq,0)$. 
\begin{enumerate}
\item If $V$ is an $\mathbb R_{\infty}$-definable set contained in a (closed) box, and $\mu$ a countably additive Keisler measure on $V$ over $\mathbb R_{\infty}$, then $\mu$ is smooth. In particular, the Keisler measure on the $\mathbb R$-definable subsets of a box $B$ induced by a Borel measure on $B$ is smooth.
\item Conversely, if $\mu$ be a smooth measure over $(\mathbb R_\infty)^k$ concentrating in a (closed) box $B$, there is a $\sigma$-additive $\mathbb R_{\infty}$-Borel measure on $B(\mathbb R_\infty)$ such that $\mu_0$ induces $\mu$ as a Keisler measure.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-6 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
The proofs are inspired and quite close to the original ones in \cite[Theorem $6.3$]{genmeasures} and \cite[Proposition 5.5]{SimonGenericallyStable}, so we will sketch the common parts and concentrate on the things we needed to add. As usual, given a subset $X$ in $\mathbb{R}^{r}_{\infty}$ we write $\overline{X}$, $X^{\mathrm{o}}$ to denote their topological closure and its open interior. \\
    
We first prove $(1)$. Let $\mu$ be a countably additive Keisler measure over $\mathbb R_{\infty}$-definable sets concentrating on an $\mathbb R_{\infty}$-definable (closed) box $B$. 
 Let $\mu'$ be any extension of $\mu$ to a global Keisler measure, then for any $\mathbb{R}_{\infty}$-definable set $X$ we have $\mu'(X)=\mu(X)$. We need to prove that for any $\mathbb M$-definable subset $D$, $\mu'(D)$ is uniquely determined by $\mu$. Let $D$ be any $\mathbb M$-definable subset inside $B$. The proof will be an induction on the o-minimal dimension of $V$. The standard map is well defined $\mathrm{st}: B(\mathbb M) \to B(\mathbb R_{\infty})$, hence we can consider $\mathrm{st}(D(\mathbb M)) \subseteq B(\mathbb R_{\infty})$. Then $st(D(\mathbb M))$ is a subset of $B(\mathbb R_{\infty})$ which, as explained in \cite[Theorem $6.3$]{genmeasures} (see the paragraph above the first claim) is $\mathbb R$-definable by definability of types in $\mathbb{R}$ (which was proved in \cite{marker-steinhorn}). In particular, $st(D(\mathbb M))$ and $st(D^c(\mathbb M))$, and their intersection $st(D(\mathbb M)) \cap st(D^{c}(\mathbb M))$ are $\mathbb R$-definable closed subsets of $B$. As in the second part of the paragraph before the first claim in \cite[Theorem $6.3$]{genmeasures}, if we let $Y, D_1$ and $D_0$ be the $\mathbb R$-definable sets such that 
$Y(\mathbb{R}_{\infty})=st(D(\mathbb M)) \cap st(D^{c}(\mathbb M)),$ $D_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{\infty})=st(D(\mathbb M)) \setminus Y$, and $D_{0}(\mathbb{R}_{\infty})=st(D^{c}(\mathbb M)) \setminus Y$, then $V$ is the disjoint union of $D_{0}$, $D_{1}$ and $Y$. \\ 

If $st(a) \in \overline{st(D(\mathbb M))}\setminus (st(D(\mathbb M)))^\circ$ then $U_{1/n}(a)$ intersects $D^c$ so in particular $a\in st(D^c)$ and both  $D_1(\mathbb R_{\infty}):=st(D(\mathbb M))\setminus st(D^c(\mathbb M))$ and $D_0(\mathbb R_{\infty}):=st(D^c(\mathbb M))\setminus st(D(\mathbb M))$ are open. 

The proof of Claim 1 in \cite[Theorem $6.3$]{genmeasures} holds and so that the set  $D_{2}:=Y \cup \left(\overline{{D_0}}\cap \overline{{D_1}}\right)$ has dimension less than $n$ because neither $Y(\mathbb{R}_{\infty})$ nor $\overline{{D_0}(\mathbb{R}_{\infty})}\cap \overline{{D_1}(\mathbb{R}_{\infty})}$ can contain 
an $\mathbb R$-definable open set. So by the induction hypothesis $\mu'$ is determined by $\mu$ on definable subsets of $D_2(\mathbb M)$.
The proof of Claim 3 in \cite[Theorem $6.3$]{genmeasures} also goes through, and proves that  for each $n \in \mathbb{N}$ we have $D(\mathbb M)\setminus U_{<1/n}(D_2(\mathbb M))=D_1(\mathbb M)\setminus U_{<1/n}(D_2(\mathbb M))$. Both $D_{1}(\mathbb M)$ and $U_{< 1/n}(D_{2}(\mathbb M))$ are $\mathbb{R}_{\infty}$-definable so that (identifying as Keisler does a measure on a definable set with the points satisfying said set in a saturated model to achieve a $\sigma$-additive extension) $\mu'(D_{1}(\mathbb M)\setminus  U_{<1/n}(D_2(\mathbb M))=\mu(D_{1}(\mathbb M)\setminus D_{2}(\mathbb M))$.  \\
Now, $D_2(\mathbb R_\infty)$ is closed, $\mathbb R$-definable and $D_2(\mathbb R_\infty)=\bigcap_{n\in \mathbb N} U_{1/n}(D_2)(\mathbb R_\infty)$. We assumed that $\mu$ was countably additive over $\mathbb R_{\infty}$ and these are all $\mathbb R_{\infty}$-definable sets so  $\mathrm{lim}_{n \rightarrow \infty} \mu(U_{1/n}(D_2)\setminus D_2)=0$. For any $n \in \mathbb{N}$
\begin{align*}
0 &\leq \mu'\Bigl(
D(\mathbb M)\cap
\bigl(U_{<1/n}(D_{2}(\mathbb M))\setminus D_2(\mathbb M)\bigr)
\Bigr)\\
&\leq \mu'\bigl(U_{1/n}(D_{2}(\mathbb M))\setminus D_2(\mathbb M)\bigr)=\mu\bigl(U_{<1/n}(D_{2}(\mathbb M))\setminus D_2(\mathbb M)
\bigr),
\end{align*}
and $\mathrm{lim}_{n \rightarrow \infty} \mu'\Bigl(
D(\mathbb M)\cap
\bigl(U_{<1/n}(D_{2}(\mathbb M))\setminus D_2(\mathbb M)\bigr)
\Bigr)=0$.\\
On the other hand, for every $n \in \mathbb{N}$, note that we have \[D(\mathbb M)\setminus D_{2}(\mathbb M)=\big( D(\mathbb M) \setminus U_{<1/n}(D_{2}(\mathbb M)) \sqcup \big( D(\mathbb M) \cap (U_{<1/n}(D_{2}(\mathbb M)) \setminus D_{2}(\mathbb M)\big).\] Consequently, we get $\mu'\big( D(\mathbb M)\setminus D_{2}(\mathbb M)\big)= \mathrm{lim}_{n \rightarrow \infty} \mu'\big( D(\mathbb M) \setminus U_{<1/n}(D_{2}(\mathbb M))\big)$.\\
Since $\mu'\big( D(\mathbb M) \setminus U_{<1/n}(D_{2}(\mathbb M))\big)=\mu\big( D_{1}(\mathbb M) \setminus U_{1<n}(D_{2}(\mathbb M))\big)$, so by the countably additive over $\mathbb{R}_{\infty}$ hypothesis, 
$\mu'(D(\mathbb M) \setminus D_{2}(\mathbb M))=\mathrm{lim}_{n \rightarrow \infty} \mu\big(D_{1}(\mathbb M)\setminus U_{<1/n}(D_{2}(\mathbb M))\big).$
As $D_1\setminus U_{<1/n}(D_2)$ is an increasing sequence of $\mathbb R_{\infty}$-definable sets converging to $D_1\setminus D_2$. 
Combining this with the fact that $\mu'\bigl(D(\mathbb M)\cap D_2(\mathbb M)\bigr)$ was uniquely determined by induction hypothesis, we get that $\mu'(D(\mathbb M))$ is uniquely determined by $\mu$, as required.\\

For $(2)$, assume now that $\mu$ is a smooth measure over $(\mathbb R_\infty)^k$ concentrating in a box $B$. As in \cite[Proposition $5.5$]{SimonGenericallyStable}, the standard part map is a Borel map from the $\sigma$-algebra generated by the definable subsets in $B(\mathbb M)$ to the Borel $\sigma$-algebra of $B(\mathbb R_{\infty})$. We take $\mu_0$ to be the pushforward of $\mu$, then $\mu_{0}$ is a $\sigma$-additive measure on $B(\mathbb R_{\infty})$. We need to show that $\mu_0(X)=\mu(X)$ for any $\mathbb R_{\infty}$-definable set, so that the Keisler measure induced by the Borel measure $\mu_0$ is indeed $\mu$.
Since $\overline X\setminus X$ always has smaller dimension than $X$, it is enough to prove the result for closed $\mathbb R_{\infty}$-definable sets $X$. Indeed, this allow us to obtain the result (as in  \cite[Proposition $5.5$]{SimonGenericallyStable}), by induction on the o-minimal dimension of $X$.\\

So let $X$ be a $\mathbb R_{\infty}$-definable closed set. By definition of $\mu_{0}$ we have $\mu_0(X(\mathbb R_{\infty}))=\mu(st^{-1}(X(\mathbb R_{\infty}))$ so we need to show that $st^{-1}(X(\mathbb M))\setminus X(\mathbb M)$ has $\mu$-measure zero. In \cite[Proposition $5.5$]{SimonGenericallyStable} this was done by taking the distance from $d_X(a)$ from any point $a$ to $X$. If $st^{-1}(X(\mathbb M))\setminus X(\mathbb M)$ then 
$d_X\left(st^{-1}\left(X\left(\mathbb M\right)\right)\setminus X\left(\mathbb M\right)\right)$ is contained in the type of $0+$ over $\mathbb R$, so $st^{-1}(X(\mathbb M))\setminus X(\mathbb M)$ must have $\mu$-measure zero since the pushforward $\mu_{d_X}$ of $\mu$ to $\mathbb R$ given by the definable function $d_X$ is also smooth so $\mu_{d_X}(0^+)$ must be zero. 

Our strategy is to repeat this proof taking into account $\infty$, and covering $st^{-1}\left(X\left(\mathbb M\right)\right)\setminus X\left(\mathbb M\right)$ with sets which are forced to have measure zero because they are pushforwards of unrealized types in $\mathbb{R}_{\infty}$. 

\begin{claim}
Let $\pi$ be any coordinate projection. Then $\mu\left(\pi^{-1}(st^{-1}(\infty))\setminus \pi^{-1}(\infty)\right)=0.$   
\end{claim}

\begin{claimproof}
If $a\in \pi^{-1}(st^{-1}(\infty))\setminus \pi^{-1}(\infty)$  then the type of $\pi(a)$ over $\mathbb{R}_{\infty}$ is the unrealized type 
$\infty^-$ given by $\{x>n: n\in \mathbb N\}\cup \{x< \infty\}$. Since the pushforward $\mu_{\pi}$ of $\mu$ under $\pi$ must be smooth,  $\mu_{\pi}(\infty^-)=0$ then $\mu(\pi^{-1}(\infty^-))=\mu\left(\pi^{-1}(st^{-1}(\infty))\setminus \pi^{-1}(\infty)\right)=0.$\end{claimproof}\\

Let $I_\infty$ be the set of indices $i$ for which the corresponding
interval defining $B$ has upper endpoint $\infty$. We write $\pi_{i}$ to denote the projection map to the $i$-th coordinate. For each
$i\in I_\infty$, let $E_{i}=\{ a \in st^{-1}(X)\setminus X \ | \ st(\pi_{i}(a))=\infty \wedge \pi_{i}(a) \neq \infty\}$. By the Claim, $E_{i}$ has $\mu$-measure zero for each $i \in I_{\infty}$. Consequently, $E=\bigcup_{i \in I_{\infty}}E_{i}$ has also $\mu$-measure zero. For each non-empty $J \subseteq I_{\infty}$ we define 
\[C_{J}=\{ x \in B\ | \ \pi_{j}(x)=\infty \ \text{for all $j \in J$ and } \ \pi_{j}(x)< \infty \text{ \ for all $j \in I_{\infty}\setminus J$}\}.\] 
Note that the $(C_{J}(\mathbb{R}_{\infty}) \ | \ J \subseteq I_{\infty})$ form a partition of $B(\mathbb{R}_{\infty})$. Let $X_{J}= C_{J} \cap X$.  Note that the set \(X_J\) is relatively closed within \(C_J\).  Furthermore, if $a \in st^{-1}(X)\setminus X$ then $a\not\in E$ implies that $st(\pi_i(a))=\infty$ only if $\pi_i(a)=\infty$, so that
\[st^{-1}(X) \setminus X \subseteq E \cup \bigcup_{J \subseteq I_{\infty}} \left(st^{-1}\left(X_{J}\right) \cap C_{J}\right)\setminus X_J.\]
Since $E$ has measure $0$, the following claim is enough to show that $st^{-1}(X) \setminus X$ has $\mu$-measure zero whenever $X$ is closed.

\begin{claim}
    $\mu(\big(st^{-1}(X_{J}) \cap C_{J}\big) \setminus X_{J})$ has $\mu$-measure zero.
\end{claim}

\begin{claimproof}
$X_{J}$ is relatively closed within $C_{J}$, and our argument is quite similar argument as in \cite[Proposition $5.5$]{SimonGenericallyStable}. We define, for $X_{J} \neq \emptyset$, the definable function  $d_{X_{J}}$  in the ordered additive structure with domain $C_{J}$ by \[d_{X_{J}}(z):= \mathrm{\inf}_{y \in X_{J}}\left\{ \max \left\{|\pi_{i}(z)-\pi_{i}(y)| \ | \  i \notin J \right\}\right\}= \mathrm{min}_{y \in X_{J}}\left\{ \max \left\{|\pi_{i}(z)-\pi_{i}(y)| \ | \  i \notin J \right\}\right\}.\] Since the infinite coordinates are fixed, note that if
$a\in\bigl(\operatorname{st}^{-1}(X_J)\cap C_J\bigr)
\setminus X_J$,
then $d_{X_J}(a)$ realizes the type $0^+$. Indeed, let $b=st(a)$ since $b \in X_{J}$ we have that $d_{X_{J}}(a) \leq \mathrm{max}_{i \notin J} |a_{i}-b_{i}|$. Every coordinate on the right-hand side of this equation has finite standard part, thus such minimum is an infinitesimal. Moreover, since $a \notin X_{J}$ and $X_{J}$ is relatively closed within $C_{J}$, then $d_{X_{J}}(a)>0$.\\ 
As before, the pushforward $d_{X_{J}}(\mu)$ is a smooth measure so by Fact \ref{fact:smoothmeasurezero} the realizations type $\{0^{+}\}$ must have $d_{X_{J}}(\mu)$-measure zero (\emph{c.f.} \cref{fact:smoothmeasurezero}). Consequently, \[\mu\bigl(
  (\operatorname{st}^{-1}(X_J)\cap C_J)\setminus X_J
\bigr)\leq \mu\bigl(d_{X_J}^{-1}(0^+(\mathbb M))\bigr)=0.\]
 \end{claimproof}
 
 
 This concludes the proof for the closed sets. The rest of the argument for arbitrary definable sets follows by induction on the o-minimal dimension of definable sets as in the last paragraph of \cite[Proposition $5.5$]{SimonGenericallyStable}.
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- claim-3: body_md -->
Let $\pi$ be any coordinate projection. Then $\mu\left(\pi^{-1}(st^{-1}(\infty))\setminus \pi^{-1}(\infty)\right)=0.$
<!-- end claim-3 -->
<!-- claimproof-3: body_md -->
If $a\in \pi^{-1}(st^{-1}(\infty))\setminus \pi^{-1}(\infty)$  then the type of $\pi(a)$ over $\mathbb{R}_{\infty}$ is the unrealized type 
$\infty^-$ given by $\{x>n: n\in \mathbb N\}\cup \{x< \infty\}$. Since the pushforward $\mu_{\pi}$ of $\mu$ under $\pi$ must be smooth,  $\mu_{\pi}(\infty^-)=0$ then $\mu(\pi^{-1}(\infty^-))=\mu\left(\pi^{-1}(st^{-1}(\infty))\setminus \pi^{-1}(\infty)\right)=0.$
<!-- end claimproof-3 -->
<!-- claim-4: body_md -->
$\mu(\big(st^{-1}(X_{J}) \cap C_{J}\big) \setminus X_{J})$ has $\mu$-measure zero.
<!-- end claim-4 -->
<!-- claimproof-4: body_md -->
$X_{J}$ is relatively closed within $C_{J}$, and our argument is quite similar argument as in \cite[Proposition $5.5$]{SimonGenericallyStable}. We define, for $X_{J} \neq \emptyset$, the definable function  $d_{X_{J}}$  in the ordered additive structure with domain $C_{J}$ by \[d_{X_{J}}(z):= \mathrm{\inf}_{y \in X_{J}}\left\{ \max \left\{|\pi_{i}(z)-\pi_{i}(y)| \ | \  i \notin J \right\}\right\}= \mathrm{min}_{y \in X_{J}}\left\{ \max \left\{|\pi_{i}(z)-\pi_{i}(y)| \ | \  i \notin J \right\}\right\}.\] Since the infinite coordinates are fixed, note that if
$a\in\bigl(\operatorname{st}^{-1}(X_J)\cap C_J\bigr)
\setminus X_J$,
then $d_{X_J}(a)$ realizes the type $0^+$. Indeed, let $b=st(a)$ since $b \in X_{J}$ we have that $d_{X_{J}}(a) \leq \mathrm{max}_{i \notin J} |a_{i}-b_{i}|$. Every coordinate on the right-hand side of this equation has finite standard part, thus such minimum is an infinitesimal. Moreover, since $a \notin X_{J}$ and $X_{J}$ is relatively closed within $C_{J}$, then $d_{X_{J}}(a)>0$.\\ 
As before, the pushforward $d_{X_{J}}(\mu)$ is a smooth measure so by Fact \ref{fact:smoothmeasurezero} the realizations type $\{0^{+}\}$ must have $d_{X_{J}}(\mu)$-measure zero (\emph{c.f.} \cref{fact:smoothmeasurezero}). Consequently, \[\mu\bigl(
  (\operatorname{st}^{-1}(X_J)\cap C_J)\setminus X_J
\bigr)\leq \mu\bigl(d_{X_J}^{-1}(0^+(\mathbb M))\bigr)=0.\]
<!-- end claimproof-4 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
\label{rmk:uniquenessRadon} The Borel measure obtained in \cref{theo: radon and smooth in R}.(2) is unique. Indeed, note that the $\sigma$-algebra generated by the $\mathbb{R}_{\infty}$-definable subsets of $B(\mathbb{R}_{\infty})$ coincides with the $\sigma$-algebra of Borel sets of the topology on $B(\mathbb{R}_{\infty})$ inherited from $\mathbb{R}_{\infty}^{r}$. We can show this by double inclusion, for the left to right direction note that this follows by cell decomposition. For the converse, let  $\mathcal{P}$ be the family of sets of the form:
\[ P_{\bar{q}}\colon=\{ x \in B(\mathbb{R}_{\infty}) \ | \ x_{i} \leq q_{i} \ \text{for  $i \leq k$}\} \qquad \bar{q} \in (\mathbb{Q}\cup \{ \infty\})^{k},\]
where the condition $x_{i} \leq \infty$ imposes no restriction. These sets are definable using parameters in $\mathbb{R}_{\infty}$ and the order, and are closed under finite intersection. Furthermore, they generate the $\sigma$-algebra of Borel subsets of $B(\mathbb{R}_{\infty})$. For the converse, the rational cuts generate the topology on each coordinate, including neighborhoods of infinity as $(q,\infty]=\mathbb{R}_{\infty} \setminus \{ x \ | \ x \leq q\}$. And the finite product $\mathbb{R}_{\infty}^{r}$ has a countable basis built from taking finite products of these intervals: $(q_{1} ,q_{2})$ or $(q,\infty]$ where $q_{1},q_{2},q \in \mathbb{Q}$. Thus, applying \cref{fact:uniquenessmeasures}, to $\mathcal{P}$ and $\mathcal{F}$ the Borel $\sigma$-algebra of $B(\mathbb{R}_{\infty})$ we obtain uniqueness of the measure given by \cref{theo: radon and smooth in R}.(2).
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- definition-14: body_md -->
\label{def:stable-completion}
The \emph{stable completion} of $X$ over $M$, denoted $\widehat X(M),$
is the set of $M$-definable stably dominated/ generically stable types concentrating on
$X$. Equivalently, by \cite[Proposition~2.9.1] {tamegeo}, $\widehat X(M)$ consists of the global types that are $M$-definable, concentrating on $X$ and are orthogonal to the value group.
<!-- end definition-14 -->
<!-- definition-15: body_md -->
\label{def:hl-topology-stable-completion} Let $f(x)$ be a fixed a regular function on $\mathbb{A}^{n}$ defined on a Zariski open set $W$. By orthogonality to $\Gamma_{\infty}(M)$ given $q \in \widehat{W}(M)$, the pushforward of $q$ along $v\circ f$ is a realized type in $\Gamma_{\infty}(M)$. We write $v(f(q))$ to indicate such element (whenever $q(x)\vdash f(x)=0$ then $v(f(q))=\infty$). Then each regular function $f(x)$ induces a natural map $r_{f}: \widehat{W\cap X}(M) \to \Gamma_{\infty}(M)$ given by $q$ goes to $v(f(q))$. And we define the topology on $\widehat{X}(M)$ to be the coarsest topology making such maps continuous, i.e. given $I$ an $M$-definable open subset of $\Gamma_{\infty}(M)$ the set $\mathbb{O}_{W,f,I}=\{ q \in \widehat{X}(M) \ | q \vdash x \in W \wedge \ v(f(q)) \in I\}$, is an open set of $\widehat{X}(M)$. Indeed, the family of sets $\mathbb{O}_{W,f,I}$ \footnote{where $W$ varies on the Zariski open sets, $f$ varies over the regular functions on $W$, and $I$ over the open $M$-deifnable intervals of $\Gamma_{\infty}(M)$.} forms a sub-basis for a topology on $\widehat{X}(M)$. We refer to this topology on $\widehat{X}(M)$ as the \emph{Hrushovski-Loeser topology}.
<!-- end definition-15 -->
<!-- definition-16: body_md -->
\label{def:basicdef} Let $M \models \mathrm{ACVF}$ and $X$ be an $M$-definable set.  If $D\subseteq X$ is $M$-definable, we write
$[D]_{\widehat X(M)}
:=
\{p\in\widehat X(M) \ | \ D(x)\in p\}.$
<!-- end definition-16 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{lem:borel-incidence-over-base}
Let $D\subseteq X$ be $M$-definable. Then
$[D]_{\widehat X(M)}$ is a Borel subset of $\widehat X(M)$.
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
By quantifier elimination in $\mathrm{ACVF}$, the set $D$ is a finite Boolean combination of sets of the
form $P(x)=0$ and 
$v(P(x))\leq v(Q(x))$,
where $P$ and $Q$ are polynomials with coefficients in $M$. Let $P \in M[\bar{x}]$. And let $r_{P}: \widehat{X}(M) \to \Gamma_{\infty}(M)$ the map defined by sending $q$ to $v(P(q))$. By the definition of the topology on $\widehat{X}(M)$, $r_{P}$ is continuous. In particular  
the sets 
\begin{align*}
[P(x)=0]_{\widehat X(M)}&=v(P)^{-1}(\{\infty\}) \ \text{and}\\
[v(P)\leq v(Q)]_{\widehat X(M)}
&=
\{q \in \widehat{X}(M):v(P(q))\leq v(Q(q))\} \end{align*}
are closed subsets of $\widehat X(M)$. It follows  that
$[D]_{\widehat X(M)}$
is Borel in $\widehat{X}(M)$.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- definition-17: body_md -->
\label{def:keislermeasure}
Let $\nu$ be a Borel probability measure on $\widehat X(M)$. We
define the map $\mathbb{B}_{x}(M) \mapsto [0,1]$ as 
$\mu_{\nu,M}(D)
:=
\nu\bigl([D]_{\widehat X(M)}\bigr)$
for every $M$-definable set $D\subseteq X$.
<!-- end definition-17 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label{lem:local-radon-barycentre-is-measure}
The map $\mu_{\nu,M}$ is a  countably additive Keisler measure over $M$.
<!-- end lemma-9 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
By Lemma~\ref{lem:borel-incidence-over-base}, the map is
well-defined. The function
$D\mapsto[D]_{\widehat X(M)}$
respects Boolean operations. Indeed, since the elements of
$\widehat X(M)$ are complete types we have that for any definable sets $D,E\subseteq X$ 
$[D\cap E]_{\widehat X(M)}=[D]_{\widehat X(M)}\cap[E]_{\widehat X(M)}$. Similarly, $[X\setminus D]_{\widehat X(M)}=\widehat X(M)\setminus[D]_{\widehat X(M)}$. Lastly, given $(E_{i})_{i \in \mathbb{N}}$ a countable family of pairwise disjoint $M$-definable sets, such that their union $E=\bigcup_{i \in \mathbb{N}}E_{i}$ is also $M$-definable, then 
$[E]_{\widehat{X}(M)}=[\bigcup_{i \in \mathbb{N}} E_{i}]_{\widehat X(M)}
=\bigcup_{i \in \mathbb{N}}[E_{i}]_{\widehat X(M)}$.  Countable additivity of $\mu_{\nu,M}$ therefore follows from  $\sigma$-additivity of $\nu$, and  $\mu_{\nu,M}(X)=\nu(\widehat X(M))=1$.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- definition-18: body_md -->
